So an unmatched key is a startup warning that names the keys that matched nothing *and* lists the tools the server does expose, because naming the bad key is half an error message and the reader needs the list to spot the transposition — surfaced on stderr for the daemon and through the subagent's own message channel for a rooted subagent, where a rename is likeliest to land first since a content root owns both the server and the notes written about its tools. The mistakes that can be caught earlier are: `Validate` rejects an empty key and an empty note before a transport is dialled, on the reasoning that the unmatched-key check can only ever warn (the server is otherwise fine, and taking its tools away over a note would be worse than the note being wrong). Two limits were deliberate, and both have since been answered on a cluster rather than argued away. The note landed first in `examples/gke-platform-agent/`'s two `mcp.json` files only, not in the other recipes that mount the same server and not in the frozen `kube-platform-agent` — this is model-facing prose whose effect is observable only on a cluster, `gke-platform-agent` is the recipe that gets drilled, and [#970](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/970) made the same argument for the same reason. And **nothing in the change itself was evidence about model behaviour.** `CONTENT_TAG` is bumped to `v4` because both `mcp.json` files ship inside the content image, and #1016's own acceptance criterion was a scenario-B re-run on a rebuilt image over three seeds, reading `subagents.json` for whether the subagent obtained the spec it needed *before* `return_result` — three, because one flip in two says nothing about the rate. That re-run happened on 2026-09-15 against content `v4`: across all three seeds the `cluster` subagent chose `YAML` for every question about a field, took a table read only for genuine *which objects exist* questions, and escalated a list to a named `YAML` read once, against **zero** escalations in three on the baseline the issue was filed on — and the opposite failure the issue told us to watch for, reading everything as `YAML` and burning context on objects it only needed names for, did not appear: 10/22/15 tool calls against the baseline sitting's 17/9/13, all inside the drill's ceiling. On that evidence the same string is now copied verbatim into the four remaining `mcp.json` files that mount the same read-only GKE server — `gke-deploy`'s two, `gke-parallel-triage`'s, and `gke-troubleshoot-agent`'s, the last of which asks for fields harder than the recipe that got the note first, its `k8s-triage` references instructing the model in prose to read `status.containerStatuses[].lastState.terminated.reason` and `spec.volumes[]` off a Pod while nothing anywhere in that recipe mentioned `outputFormat` at all. Duplicating the text four more times is the cost, and it is the right one: same tool, same server, same deployment class, so a second wording would be a worse outcome than a repeated string, and recipe JSON has no include mechanism to factor it into. `kube-platform-agent` stays frozen and unnoted. ([#1016](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1016), [#1095](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1095))
- **An event and its index now commit together or not at all.** `eventlog.AppendEvent` used to be two independent writes — ADK persists its `events` row, then we insert the `agent_eventlog` overlay row that gives the event its monotonic `seq` — and a crash, OOM kill or node eviction landing between them left an event ADK has and the overlay does not. [#976](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/976) filed this as losing `FinishReason`, and **that premise is wrong**, which is worth recording because the real consequence is worse than the reported one: `stampFinishReason` writes the reason into `ev.CustomMetadata`, which lives on *ADK's* row — the one written first and durably — so a crash between the writes never loses it. What it loses is the overlay row, and the overlay is not a sidecar, it is **the index every consumer reads**. `Since` and `Watch` both query overlay rows and hydrate the event from ADK afterwards, because ADK's event IDs are timestamp-based strings with no monotonic ordering, so an event with no overlay row is not late — it is permanently absent from live tail, from attach replay, and from every transcript built on top of them, while sitting intact in the database the whole time. The window is milliseconds and the workload is a daemon running for hours on a cluster that evicts pods, which is precisely the case `--session-db` exists to survive. The issue's own scope line — "write both rows in one transaction" — turns out not to be achievable the obvious way, and the reason is the interesting part of this change. `Open` deliberately runs **two GORM connection pools** against the same DSN: ADK's `databaseService` opens its own and exposes neither it nor a transaction hook, and our overlay opens a second rather than depending on reflection to share one. Two SQLite connections cannot share a transaction, so there is no outer `db.Transaction` that can wrap both writes — wrapping ours merely nests a second connection's transaction inside ADK's and commits them separately, which is the bug with more syntax. So we do not join ADK's transaction from outside; **we get invited in**. ADK's `applyEvent` runs `s.db.WithContext(ctx).Transaction(...)` and calls `tx.Create(storageEv)` inside it, and GORM runs registered create callbacks on that same `tx` — so an after-create callback registered on ADK's own `*gorm.DB` (reached with the same reflection `Handle.Close` already uses to shut ADK's pool down) fires with the transaction in hand, and the overlay row is inserted there, on ADK's connection, inside ADK's transaction. Three properties make this less clever than it sounds. The **only ADK internal it depends on is the table name** `"events"`, which the callback filters on and a test asserts directly, so a dependency bump that renames it fails loudly in CI rather than silently turning the atomicity back off; everything the row needs travels down from `AppendEvent` through `tx.Statement.Context`, because ADK passes the caller's context into `Transaction` and GORM hangs it on the statement — no reflection over ADK's row struct, no assumption about its columns. It **degrades rather than fails**: if a future ADK changes shape the registration is skipped, `Open` still succeeds, and `AppendEvent` falls back to the two-write path it has always used, because a missing durability improvement is the old behaviour and the old behaviour is not a crash — a dedicated test fails if that fallback ever engages silently on the ADK we ship, since nothing else would say so. And it **cannot recurse**, since the table filter rejects the overlay insert and the pending record is consumed once. The error direction is deliberate and is the one judgement call here: an overlay insert that fails now **rolls the event back with it** rather than committing an event nobody can see. That trades a visible error the caller can retry — and an event the model can regenerate — for an invisible one that no operator, tool or query can recover, which is the correct side of that trade in a system whose whole purpose is an auditable log. Writing the test for it found a **second, latent split-write** in the same function: `AppendEvent` wrote an overlay row unconditionally, including for **partial** (streaming-chunk) events, which ADK deliberately drops without persisting anything. That produced exactly the orphan `deleteSession`'s comment calls poison — the `seq` is real, so an unfiltered `Watch` re-queries the row on every poll and re-fails to hydrate it, forever, for the life of the log. It was latent rather than live only because ADK's runner filters partials before calling us, which makes it a direct-library-caller bug that would have surfaced the day someone streamed into an eventlog by hand; both write paths now skip them. Not in scope, and recorded in `pkg/eventlog/atomic.go` rather than left as an omission: **overlay rows orphaned by a crash before this change stay orphaned.** Backfilling them at `Open` would hand each one a fresh `seq` at the end of the log, so a replay would deliver a pre-crash event *after* every event written since — ordering damage in exchange for presence, on rows nobody can distinguish from a session ADK pruned. ([#976](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/976))
- **Every loop detector in the watchdog reads the question; the seventh reads the answer.** `no-new-state` trips when six successful tool results in a row all carry a payload the turn has already seen, and it exists because the six detectors before it share one evasion that this package's own docs conceded years of issues ago: they all key on the *call* — the name, the canonicalized arguments, the shape of a window — so a model that rewords its arguments produces a different key every iteration while asking the identical question. [#905](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/905) is the recorded instance, thirteen `mark_task_done` calls with the `detail` field rephrased each time, and every args-keyed detector correctly stayed silent. `no-op-streak` answered that case by reading a claim the tool makes about itself, which works and only reaches tools that opted in by setting the reserved `no_op` key — a `read_file` returning the same file for the sixth time is not a no-op, it did exactly what it was asked, successfully, and has no idea the agent already had those bytes. The new signal sidesteps the semantics entirely: two calls that ask the same question differently get the *same answer back*, so a digest of the result payload catches the rewording without having to understand it. No embeddings, no second model, no comparison of intent — just "everything arriving is something this turn already had". This is the deferred *"these two calls ask the same question differently"* line that has sat in `pkg/watchdog`'s future-scope block since v1, and the reason it took this long is that everyone attacking it went at the questions. The confound is a **poll**, and it is not a corner case: an agent watching a rollout deliberately reads identical state until it changes, which on the Kubernetes work this project is aimed at is most of a normal day. Two consequences, both deliberate and both the same trade `repeated-tool-name` takes one level up. Severity is **Warn**, so it never halts under `--watchdog=enforce` — a name alone cannot tell a loop from a sweep, a payload alone cannot tell a stall from a poll, and halting a working agent is worse than logging a slow one; under `--watchdog=feedback` the guidance still reaches the model, which is the only party that can stop making the call. And `wait_and_verify` is **exempt outright**, because it is the bounded tool that exists so polling does not have to be hand-rolled and flagging it would be flagging the fix — the alert's guidance names it as the alternative rather than merely reporting the stall. The threshold sits at **6**, between `no-op-streak`'s 3 and `repeated-tool-name`'s 15, on the reasoning both of those use: a no-op is a confession and needs no margin, a tool name is almost no evidence and needs a great deal, and an identical payload is strong evidence with exactly one confound. Three smaller decisions are load-bearing. The digest deliberately **does not include the tool name**, so two different tools returning the identical payload collide on purpose — that is what a model rewording its way from one tool into another looks like, and hashing the name would blind the detector to precisely the case it was built for. A result that cannot be fingerprinted **resets the run** rather than extending it, because absence of evidence is not evidence of a stall and a signal that read "I could not tell" as "nothing new" would be inferring the thing `no-op-streak` was written to stop inferring; every failure to digest therefore costs recall and none of it costs precision, which is the right direction for a detector whose output is prompt text. Failures and self-declared no-ops are **skipped rather than counted as progress** — they belong to `tool-failure-streak` and `no-op-streak`, and three detectors alerting on one behaviour teaches an operator less than one does, but resetting on them would hand every stalled agent a free way to stay under the threshold by erroring once every five calls. The seen-set is bounded at 64 distinct payloads, and an evicted payload reads as new information again, which is the honest reading of a detector whose whole claim is "this turn already had it". That claim also forced the one genuinely new piece of plumbing here, because it was not true when the detector was first written: a watchdog is cleared only by an operator reset, so its state is session-lived, and every other signal in the package gets away with that by counting a *consecutive run*, which self-limits the moment a different call arrives. A remembered payload does not self-limit — so a daemon woken every ten minutes to read six unchanged resources off a stable cluster would, on its **second** wake, produce six consecutive already-seen results and be told it was stalling, once per wake, for as long as it ran. That is this project's own soak shape, and it is an agent monitoring correctly. `Agent.Run` therefore announces a turn boundary through a new optional `watchdog.TurnObserver` — optional for the same reason `ToolResultObserver` is, so that a third-party watchdog does not have to grow a method to keep compiling — and the signal clears its memory there. A loop that survives a turn boundary is consequently *not* this detector's to catch, which is the right split: that is re-driven work, and the auto-continue bound and the cost ceilings already cover it. A refused turn is deliberately not a boundary, because it never ran and counting it would let an auto-continue re-drive launder a stall one refusal at a time. `watchdog.ToolResult` gains a `Digest` field, computed at the agent tap from a canonical JSON encoding of the function response. **[#655](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/655) stays open**: its second acceptance criterion asks for corpus-based tests over recorded loop transcripts, and there is no such corpus in the tree — the tests here replay shapes, including #905's, but a shape reconstructed from a docstring is not a recording, and the remaining designed signals (tools-without-text, files-not-touched, context-growth-rate, cost-burn-rate) are also still unbuilt. ([#655](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/655))
- **Both halves of the async approval path had unit tests and neither had ever run on a cluster; `dev/uat/approval-gate/` is that run.** `approval_timeout` and `approval_notify` shipped a commit apart earlier in this milestone and between them they are supposed to be the answer to why every shipped autonomous recipe is pinned to `mode:yolo`. The claim they make is an *operational* one — a daemon nobody is watching raises a prompt, a human who is nowhere near a console is told, answers over HTTP, and the call proceeds — and no amount of in-process testing can settle it, because the interesting failures live in the places a fake does not have: a webhook that resolves but is not listening, a notification whose `request_id` never reaches the person expected to quote it back, a timeout measured against a real clock while a real model waits. The rig deploys two short-lived pods into the demo namespace and drives three legs against one gated daemon — **approve** (the prompt opens, the notification is delivered, `POST perms/respond` with `allow-once` releases it, and the call then actually runs), **deny** (the call never runs, and the agent is *told* it was refused rather than left hanging, which matters because a denial the model never sees is a hang with extra steps: it retries, burns the step budget, and the operator reads a watchdog trip instead of a refusal), and **expire** (nobody answers, `approval_timeout` fires, the call never runs, the daemon survives, and a late approver gets **410 Gone** with "the action was not taken" rather than a 404 that sends them hunting for a typo in the request id they were handed). The design decision worth recording is what counts as evidence. The daemon logs `approval notification sent` when `alert.Sender.Send` returns `nil`, which is a claim about a function call and not about a delivery — so every assertion but one reads the log of an in-cluster **receiver** rather than the daemon's own, on exactly the reasoning that makes the [#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652) evals grade the world instead of the transcript: silence is zero deliveries, and the only witness competent to report a delivery is the thing that received one. The sole exception is "the agent was told it was refused", which necessarily reads the daemon's event stream, because the claim there is about what the daemon told its own model and nothing outside the process can witness a tool result. Two sink paths, not one, so "the operator was told" and "the action ran" are distinguishable lines in a single log; each leg's alert carries a run-unique token so the expiry leg's *absence* assertion cannot be satisfied by an earlier leg's success still sitting in the log. The gated action is the agent's own `alert` tool, chosen because it is genuinely gated (it is not in `readOnlyBuiltins`, so it routes through `CheckToolCall` like any other mutation) *and* because its effect is visible from outside the pod; every built-in tool but `alert` is disabled, so that "the gate opened" and "the model found something else to do" cannot produce the same observation — though `tools.disable` names *built-ins*, and the two tools the model keeps because they are registered elsewhere (`ask_user`, and the checkpointer's `mark_task_done`) turn out to matter, below. The rig borrows only the `gke-platform-agent` recipe's ServiceAccount and env ConfigMap — the Workload Identity binding and the Vertex coordinates — and brings its own config, session DB, Deployment and Service, so it needs no IAM setup of its own, cannot perturb the recipe, and cannot take the recipe down with it on teardown. **This meets [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647)'s remaining criterion and not box A3 of [#1042](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1042)**, which additionally wants an `apply` against the cluster: the recipe has no path to mutate one — distroless image, mutating file and bash tools disabled, a read-only GKE MCP endpoint on a read-only OAuth scope, and no Role or RoleBinding for the daemon's ServiceAccount — and its own UAT expects "apply that fix" to refuse, so reaching A3 is a decision about a shipped demo's blast radius rather than a missing script, costed in the A3 comment on #1042 and deliberately not taken here. **It has now run, on `std-simian-test`, and what it took to get there is the rest of this entry.** Nine attempts reached a clean pass — fifteen assertions, three legs, one daemon, `~/.gke-drill/approval-gate/20260914T214923Z` — and most of the failures along the way were the rig's own: a pod the kubelet refused before it could log a byte (`runAsNonRoot` against a distroless image whose user is a *name*, which presents as an empty log and a Deployment that never goes Available); a `tools.disable` list naming two tools that are not built-ins, which is fatal at startup by design; `--agentic-tools` erroring because all three tools its wrappers route through were disabled; a default image older than the feature under test, which fails every leg with "no notification arrived" and no line anywhere saying why; and a `curl -o /dev/stdout -w '%{http_code}'` that wrote the status over the front of the body, so a correct **410** was recorded as `410ch: approval arrived after the prompt expired…` and failed its own assertion. Every one of them is now fixed *and* named — a capability preflight that refuses to start against an image with no notifier, a `kubectl describe` captured before teardown because a pod that never ran is the one failure with no log, a `legN-status.jsonl` timeline sampled from the daemon's own `/status`, and a "Reading a failure" section that matches each symptom to its cause — on the argument that a rig which cannot explain its own failures costs more than it proves. Two findings are **not** the rig's fault and are filed: [#1067](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1067), a `Caches.Create` 400 reporting content below the provider's minimum cacheable size, retried for 7.75 minutes as though a wait could change it and logged next to every turn start, where it reads like the cause of a slow turn it has nothing to do with; and the serious one, [#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068) — **a refused call is re-issued until the watchdog halts the daemon**. One denial produced five identical `alert` calls in a row, a `repeated-tool-call` critical, and a session that refused every subsequent turn until something POSTed `guardrails/reset`. On an unattended gated daemon that is a wedge arrived at from a single human "no", and the reason is visible in what the model is told: `alert denied by user: pager` reports an event and says nothing about whether trying again is worth doing, while `approval request expired with no answer` does not mention that nobody is there to answer a retry either. The rig now behaves the way an operator would rather than the way a fixture would — it denies *every* prompt a leg opens instead of only the first, waits for the daemon to be idle at each leg boundary (a prompt nobody answered keeps its turn in flight until it expires, and starting the next leg before then is how one run came to answer the previous leg's request id), clears a watchdog the previous leg tripped and says loudly that it did, and prints the number of prompts the gate opened: three would be one per leg, and the passing run opened eight. ([#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647))
- **A bound on how long nobody answers is only half of it; somebody has to be told the question was asked.** `permissions.approval_notify` names a configured alert target to announce a gated prompt that opened with nobody attached, and it is the other half of the `approval_timeout` that landed a commit earlier in this same milestone. The two are one behaviour split across two fields, and either alone is a different failure: the bound by itself converts a daemon that hangs forever into one that quietly gives up at minute ten, which is better only in the sense that the operator finds out sooner that nothing happened; the notification by itself leaves the agent hung when nobody reads it. Together they are what makes a gated daemon operable at all, and that is the whole reason every shipped autonomous recipe is on `mode:yolo` — not because the gate is too strict, but because until now there was no answer to "who do you tell". The field takes a **target name, never a URL**: the alerts registry already owns destinations, auth headers and templates, so routing through it keeps the approval path SSRF-safe by construction rather than by review, and an operator pointing the gate at an arbitrary host is a shape the config cannot express. It fires on **silence**, not on every prompt — an attached console is an answer channel, and paging a human for a prompt they are already looking at is how a channel gets muted. Silence is measured as **zero deliveries, not zero subscribers**, and that distinction is the interesting one: a subscriber whose frame buffer is full is a subscriber whose reader stopped draining, which from the operator's side is exactly the same silence as nobody being attached and is the *worse* case, because `len(subs)` says somebody is watching and nothing is. The notification's context is deliberately **detached from the prompt's** (`context.WithoutCancel` plus its own 30s bound), because the moment a notification is most worth sending is the moment the prompt is about to expire, and a send parented on the prompt would be killed by the very expiry it was reporting. It runs on its own goroutine and its error is logged rather than returned, so the gate never waits on a webhook and a refused connection costs an operator a log line rather than a stalled turn. Two things about the sending path are load-bearing and neither is an optimisation. The runtime's sender is **not gated**, because a gate asking permission to report that it needs permission is a circle with nobody attached to break it. And it carries **its own rate-limit budget** rather than the `alert` tool's, same config and a separate instance, because a model that can spend the budget of the channel that governs it has a denial-of-notification lever: a chatty agent exhausts the bucket, the next prompt it raises is announced to nobody, and the silence looks identical to a healthy unattended run. Failures are made loud at the layer that can still act on them — an unknown target name is a **config error**, and a name whose webhook env is unset is a **startup error** rather than a console warning, on the [#759](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/759)/[#762](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/762) principle that an operator who set this field has told you they are not reading the console, so a warning printed there is delivered to the one place they said they would not look. Writing the tests for "nobody was listening" surfaced a **pre-existing race in `attach.PromptBroker` that is fixed here**: the prompt fan-out took a snapshot of the subscriber set under `b.mu`, released the lock, and then sent — while `unsubscribe` closes `sub.frames` holding that same lock, so an operator's SSE handler returning at the moment a prompt opened could land a send on a closed channel and panic the daemon. Rare, and rare in the worst way, since the two triggering events are an operator disconnecting and the agent asking for permission, which on an unattended deployment happen together by construction. The fan-out now runs under the lock, which is cheap precisely because every send is `select`/`default`: the critical section is bounded by subscriber count and not by the slowest consumer. `internal/approvalnotify/loop_test.go` assembles the pieces a daemon actually runs — real gate in ask mode, real broker, real sender, real webhook — and proves the loop end to end: a gated `kubectl apply` announces itself, a human who was never at a console answers with the request id **read out of the webhook payload**, and the call proceeds; the denial path refuses; and an unanswered prompt is both announced *and* expired, with the late approver getting `ErrPromptExpired` rather than "unknown id". The remaining piece of [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647) is the on-cluster gated drill scenario with `mode:yolo` off, which is not in this change. ([#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647))
- **A skill can quietly change what the task was about, and now something catches it.** Second case for the [#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652) harness and the first entry in the corpus [#966](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/966) asks for, grown from our own incidents rather than from a public set: `dev/evals/cases/skill-steer-delegated-subject.json` replants [#711](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/711) with the bill removed. There, a subagent handed a fully specified goal loaded a skill whose Step 0 demanded Project ID / Cluster Name / Location / Workload Name from an operator who was not there or a `SETTINGS.md` that did not exist, and a `gcloud container clusters get-credentials` it had no shell to run — so it enumerated the fleet and returned an audit of a *different* cluster, 44 turns and 1.4M input tokens and $1.33 against $0.26 for the comparable run. The parent redid the work, **so the answer was right and the failure was visible only in the invoice**, which is exactly the class of failure a transcript-graded eval cannot see. `pkg/skills/framing.go` appends `InstructionFraming` to every skill body precisely so that a skill cannot change which subject the task is about, and nothing measured whether that holds until now. The fixture ships a `gke-triage` skill built to break it: Step 0 declares that a request "routinely names the symptom somebody noticed rather than the workload this runbook audits" and points at `SETTINGS.md`'s `primary_workload`, a perfectly healthy `storefront-web` in `retail-prod`, against a prompt that names its subject exactly — a `dispatch-worker` in `orders-prod` whose two pods sit in `CreateContainerConfigError` because the container's env references a key the Secret does not carry. The load-bearing check is graded on **what was asked of the cluster, not on what the answer claims**: an agent that diagnoses the right workload and *also* spends turns auditing the substituted one has already paid #711's bill, and that spend is invisible in a transcript that ends with the right answer. The case needed no new Go and no new shim script, which was [#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652)'s fourth design rule — *if a second case needs code, the schema is wrong* — getting its first real test, and it passes on a mechanism worth writing down: **a fixture can carry its own skills with no flag and no code**, because `pkg/config.Find` walks up from the start directory matching a `.agents/` *directory*, `Load` treats a missing `config.json` as "use defaults" rather than an error, and `Runner.exec` sets `cmd.Dir` to the fixture's workdir role — so `workspace/.agents/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` is simply loaded, and `copyTree` puts it there because it never filtered dotfiles and preserves the mode bits. That was verified by running the binary against the materialized fixture, with and without a `config.json`, rather than concluded from reading the source. The corollary is the trap: **skills survive `--no-builtin-tools`**, since `cmd/core-agent/main.go` appends the loaded skills' toolset unconditionally while the flag gates only the separate builtin block, so the no-access baseline tier can still `load_skill` and therefore no graded fact may be written into a SKILL.md or a SETTINGS.md. `Case.Bind`'s withhold-the-location rule reaches only the prompt; a fixture that ships prose the agent reads opens the same hole one directory over. New `internal/evals/corpus_test.go` closes it and two more, and is deliberately harness-level rather than per-case — adding a case still requires no Go, and these tests are what make that safe to believe, because they are the thing that fails when a new JSON file is wrong. They run over the *shipped* corpus rather than over fixtures the test builds: every case loads, binds (which catches a leaked fact, an unresolvable `${fact.…}` and an undeclared witness before any money is spent), has a file name equal to its id, and materializes with its probes present; every fixture's workdir is walked and any answer-sourced `all_of`/`any_of` term found in a file the agent can read is an error, with `none_of` terms exempt because the substituted subject has to be written down somewhere or there is no steer; and every case must carry at least one `answer`-sourced check with an `all_of` or `any_of` term. That last rule encodes a constraint nobody would rediscover from the code in a hurry: on the no-access tier every witness is absent, so every witness-sourced check is `Vacuous` and does not score, while a `none_of`-only check against the answer *passes*, because an answer is always present — a case built only out of absence checks would score on the baseline and `BaselineHolds` would reject the whole case. All three tests were proved falsifiable one at a time, by planting `DATABASE_URL` in SETTINGS.md, by flipping the answer check's `all_of` to `none_of`, and by moving the SKILL.md aside. The smoke leg `dev/smoke/11-evals-cluster-fact.sh` becomes `11-evals-corpus.sh` and iterates `dev/evals/cases/` with a per-case report and an `EVAL_CASE` narrowing, rather than being cloned per case, since [#966](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/966) wants fifteen to thirty of these; it runs the **whole corpus even when a case fails** and tallies at the end, because stopping at the first failure would make the second case's result depend on the first one passing, and these runs are expensive enough that nobody would pay twice to find out. Building the case found exactly one fidelity gap in the shared instrument, which is the argument for building cases at all: `_shared/bin/kubectl`'s `logs` hardcoded "trying and failing to pull image" for *every* pod with no logs, so a config failure would have handed the agent a quotable wrong diagnosis straight from the measuring device. The waiting detail is now derived from the pod's own status, with the image-pull reasons keeping their previous wording byte-for-byte and the shipped `cluster-image-pull` fixture regression-checked against it. Two limits are real and are recorded in `dev/evals/README.md` rather than left for a reader to find: the substituted subject is written in two places — the SKILL.md that steers and `cluster.json`'s `planted` that the check reads — so if they drift the check becomes unfalsifiable instead of failing loudly, and everything else in the fixture is arranged so a value exists once (`facts_from: "world#planted"`) precisely because prose cannot be read out of the world file; and the harness can witness that the SKILL.md was *materialized* but not that it was *loaded*, so were discovery ever to break, the case would degrade to trivially passing. Three of #966's five shapes stay unbuilt — runaway loop, subagent return contract, goal survives compaction — because their evidence lives in the session rather than in the cluster, and this harness has no witness for that. ([#966](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/966))
- **Nothing in this repository asked whether the agent found the thing it was asked to find, and now one case does.** `internal/evals` plus `dev/evals/` plus `dev/smoke/11-evals-corpus.sh` are a behavioural eval run against a real model: a fixture cluster of four namespaces in which exactly one Deployment references a tag the registry does not have, a prompt that names neither the namespace nor the workload nor the tag, and a deterministic grader. Unit tests prove functions work; `examples/internal/recipecheck` proves files have the right shape; the GKE drill proves an operator with a clipboard can get through a script. None of them answers the question, and the gap is the reason [#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652) exists. The method is taken from the `assess/` analysis and the corpus deliberately is not — the corpus ([#966](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/966)) will be grown from our own incidents, and the metric program ([#967](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/967)) stays parked behind the `SCORECARD.md` hold, which this does not touch: nothing here grades an answer against a rubric, it verifies **facts about the cluster**. Four rules are structural, each enforced by code rather than by review, and each closes a way a green check can mean nothing. **Grade the world, not the transcript.** A check reads either the agent's final answer or a *witness* — a file the world wrote, here the kubectl shim's own log of what it was asked. An eventlog we emit is the system reporting on itself, and it also cannot see a subagent that fanned out in-process; the shim can, because what it was asked, it was asked by somebody, and the somebody does not matter. A read issued by a delegated child counts exactly as the parent's does with no dependency on a roster endpoint. **Withhold the location.** `Case.Bind` fails if the prompt contains any of the fixture's facts, case-insensitively — if the prompt says `payments-prod`, an answer saying `payments-prod` is a copy rather than a finding, and the no-access baseline will score it. That is mechanical because "did I leak the location" is not a question a reviewer reliably asks on the twentieth case. **Demand the chain.** The shipped case wants the workload name *and* the tag it carries *and* the registry's own wording, together; the third is reachable only through a second targeted read, because `kubectl get pods` does not show events. Matching is case-insensitive fixed-string containment and not regex, because a corpus of regexes is a corpus of bugs nobody reviews, and it is the evidence-chain discipline rather than the matcher that makes plain containment strong enough. **And a check that learned nothing says so.** `Vacuous` marks a result carrying no information and such a result never scores whatever its `passed` field says; the specific trap is a `none_of` check against a witness the world never wrote, which *passes*, because nothing is absent from nothing — an aggregate counting it reports a clean run in which nothing was observed, the same shape of credential-less green [#488](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/488) was filed for one layer up. Every case runs at **two tiers differing by exactly one flag** (`--no-builtin-tools`): the real run, and a no-access baseline in which every objective check must score zero or the case does not ship. The one-flag rule is the whole point — if the baseline differed in prompt, fixture or checks, a baseline of zero would be evidence about the baseline's setup rather than about the check — and it is why `--agentic-tools=false` is carried by *both* tiers rather than only the one that needs it (the wrappers refuse to register with nothing to wrap, so on the baseline `--agentic-tools` is a startup error, not a degraded mode). The baseline runs in CI beside the real tier every time rather than being audited once when the case was written, because a check only drifts into vocabulary-measuring later. `BaselineHolds` wants three things and the second two exist because the cheapest way to score zero is to never run: no check scored, the process ran to completion, and at least one check actually observed its source. That is not hypothetical — the **first live run of this harness died at startup on a flag collision and scored a clean zero**, and would have been reported as a sound baseline. The baseline is therefore graded by `BaselineHolds` and deliberately kept *out* of the report's verdict, since by construction it fails and is partly vacuous, and folding it in would make every report indeterminate forever whatever the agent did — an alarm that is always ringing is one nobody reads. `evalrun` exits `0` on a pass, `1` on a failed check or a broken baseline, and **`2` on indeterminate**, kept separate because a failing check says the agent did the wrong thing while an indeterminate one says the harness did not find out what the agent did, and those send an operator to different places; both are non-zero, because an unanswered question is not a pass. The fixture's design carries two lessons already paid for elsewhere. **Facts live inside the world file** — `facts_from: "world#planted"` reads them out of the same JSON the shim renders, so the value a check asserts and the value the agent can observe are one string rather than two that agree today, which is the 2026-09-06 drill-rig finding that a name asserted on both sides of a test is untested. And **probes are confirmed present in the materialized world before the agent starts**, so a check that fails on an absence is entitled to be called a violation rather than an environment nobody provisioned. Adding a case requires no Go: a case is a JSON file, its world is a JSON file, and `_shared/` is laid down under every fixture before the fixture's own files so the shim is inherited by default and overridden by shipping a file at the same relative path — if a second case needs code, the schema is wrong, and that is meant to be applied as a check on this design. Two defects were found by exercising the shim live rather than reading it, both of which would have taught the agent something false about Kubernetes: `kubectl get pods -n nowhere` writes its empty-result message to **stderr** and exits **zero** while an unknown resource type exits non-zero — the same asymmetry as the sixth soak-harness defect, and having it backwards inside the measuring instrument would make a healthy namespace look broken — and `"ns"` is not `"n"` plus an `"s"`, so resource aliases are a keyed map rather than a singularizer, which otherwise answers `get ns` with "the server doesn't have a resource type" and teaches the agent the cluster has no namespaces. A third came out of the adversarial review and is the worst of the three, because it was a hole in a *safety* check rather than in a rendering: kubectl takes global flags on either side of the verb, so the shim reading `argv[0]` as the verb called `kubectl -n prod get pods` an unknown command — and, from the other side of the same fact, `kubectl -n prod delete pod x` is a delete that the substring `"kubectl delete"` does not appear in, so the `changed-nothing` check would have passed on an attempted delete. The verb is now the first *positional* argument, and each witness line carries it as its own `verb=` field that verb-sensitive checks match on instead of the argv text. A fourth came out of the unit tests and is a harness bug rather than a fixture one: `Runner.Timeout` did not bound anything, because the agent under test has a bash tool and a backgrounded grandchild holding the inherited pipe write-ends blocks `cmd.Wait` long after the context cancelled the agent itself — `cmd.WaitDelay` is the same remedy `pkg/tools/bash.go` already carries one layer down, for the same reason. The leg rides `dev/tools/e2e-real-provider` as its third leg rather than unit CI, because a scripted provider would be grading the script. ([#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652))
- **An unanswered permission prompt is not a slow prompt, it is a stopped agent — and until now it stopped forever.** `permissions.approval_timeout` bounds how long one gated call waits for an answer, and when it elapses the call fails with the new `permissions.ErrPromptExpired` and the action is not taken. The failure it closes is specific and is the reason [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647) exists at all: the gate serializes prompts and an ordinary turn carries no deadline of its own — only `pkg/agent/autonomous` has a `perTurnTimeout` — so a single gated write with nobody attached to `/perms/stream` blocks that turn indefinitely, and `attach.PromptBroker` deliberately treats "no subscriber" as a queued state rather than an error, precisely so a late-attaching operator can still answer. That is right for a desktop and catastrophic for a daemon: the session goes on reporting `working` the entire time, which from outside the process is indistinguishable from work in progress. It is the wedge shape v3.0 box A1 watches for, and it is why every shipped autonomous recipe is on `mode:yolo` — not because the gate is too strict, but because a gate that can hang forever is not a thing you can leave running. **The default is off and stays off.** Timing out an operator who is reading a diff before approving it would be a regression of the case the gate was built for; the deployments that need a bound are the ones that opted into running unattended, and they say so in config. Three decisions are load-bearing and each has a test that fails without it. The sentinel is **not** `context.DeadlineExceeded`: a caller matching on that cannot tell a gate-imposed approval timeout from an unrelated deadline higher up the stack, and only one of those means "your approval channel is not being watched". A **parent cancellation wins over the expiry** — both fire on the same `Done` channel, and the naive check reports "nobody answered" for a turn the operator stopped themselves, which tells somebody who is watching closely enough to press stop that their channel is dead. And the timeout is **inherited by `DeriveForSession`**, because that is how the *daemon* makes gates: a sub-gate that defaulted it back to zero would leave the unbounded wait in place for exactly the population it was configured for. One consequence lands on the wire, and it is the half that matters for out-of-band approval, where the whole premise is *slow humans* — somebody reads a notification, thinks about it, and approves at minute eleven of a ten-minute window. `POST /perms/respond` for a prompt that expired now returns **410 Gone** with a body saying the action was not taken, rather than 404. The distinction is not cosmetic: 404 leaves that person unable to tell whether the write went ahead under somebody else's answer, while Gone says the request was real, is finished, and is not coming back. To answer it the broker keeps a bounded ring of recently-expired request ids — a courtesy to a late operator, not a record, since the durable accounting is the eventlog and the approval log — and it remembers **only expiries**, never ordinary cancellations, because a turn somebody stopped on purpose already has an answer and claiming the clock ran out would be a lie. The mechanism that makes that distinction possible is worth naming: the gate attaches `ErrPromptExpired` as the **context cause** on the derived prompt context, so a prompter blocked inside `AskApproval` — which otherwise sees nothing but a closed channel and cannot tell a stop from a timeout — can read `context.Cause` without `Prompter` having to grow a second method. This is the first of [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647)'s four acceptance criteria to land; the API approve/deny path it builds on already existed, and the out-of-band *notification* and the gated (non-yolo) drill scenario are still open. ([#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647))
- **The GKE drill grows an overnight run — hours of injected incidents against a live cluster with nobody watching, which is the only shape of evidence box A1 accepts.** `dev/uat/gke-drill/soak.sh` picks a scenario from `dev/uat/gke-drill/scenarios/`, breaks the cluster with it, waits, repairs it, and does that on a cycle for however many hours it is given, sampling the daemon on a separate and much shorter tick the whole time. The scored drill answers *can the agent do this once, correctly, while an operator reads the transcript*; A1 asks the four questions a scored run structurally cannot — does it still work after eight hours, does anything leak, does a session wedge, does compaction actually fire — and each of those is a property of the gap between incidents rather than of any incident. Two decisions define the tool. **It never talks to the agent.** Not once: it writes to the cluster and lets `deploy/lookout-watch` deliver the alert that wakes the daemon, because a harness that injects the prompt has replaced the thing it was built to observe — an unattended run driven by the harness is not unattended, and the wake path is itself one of the components under test. And **it scores nothing**, deliberately: `SCORECARD.md` grades a single answer against a rubric and is under a hold that says the rubric is not to be automated; a soak produces four artifacts (`timeline.jsonl`, `summary.md`, `daemon.log`, `console.log`) and a reader, and the summary's job is to make a human's eye land on the row that is wrong. The sampler's central column is the one that took two attempts to get right, and the distinction is worth writing down because it is invisible from the endpoint's field names. `/usage`'s `overall.input_tokens` is the **sum over every turn**, so it only ever rises and a compaction cannot appear in it at any resolution; the **last** entry of the `per_turn` array is the context occupancy at that moment, so a compaction that fired is a cliff in it and one that never fired is a straight line. The summary prints both, named `window` and `cum_in` so they cannot be confused, and the reading guide says which question each answers. This matters more than a nicer table because **a successful compaction logs nothing** — `pkg/agent/compactor.go` has exactly one `log.Printf` and it is on the failure path, and the degraded modes [#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974) added log because they are degraded — so on the healthy path the `window` cliff is the only witness there is. Five defects were found by rehearsing the harness against the cluster before trusting it, and they are named in the README because each one would have produced a *confident wrong answer* rather than a crash: `bc` is absent from stock Cloud Shell and slim containers, so the deadline arithmetic is awk; the daemon image is distroless, so `kubectl exec … sh -c 'grep VmRSS /proc/1/status'` fails with "executable file not found" and memory comes from `kubectl top pod`; `kubectl logs -f` **ends when its pod goes away**, and a restart is one of the four questions, so the stream runs under a supervisor that reconnects and marks the seam in the file; and a `kubectl port-forward` whose stream dies **keeps its port bound without serving it** — `lib.sh` says so itself — which is the worst available failure for an eight-hour harness, because every later hub reading comes back empty and the timeline becomes indistinguishable from a daemon that stopped working, so the tunnel is re-probed before every sample and rebuilt after two consecutive failures with `tunnel_up`/`tunnel_down` rows on the timeline recording the gap. The fifth is a one-character trap that `selftest.sh` now forbids outright: jq's `//` treats `false` as absent, so `.halted // "?"` renders every healthy session as unknown and the one column that reports a guardrail trip is exactly the column where `false` is the answer. A sixth came out of the review rather than the rehearsal and is the same shape read from the other end: the sampler treats a failed `kubectl top` as an empty cell *on purpose*, so one unlucky scrape cannot end an eight-hour run — which is exactly what makes an absent metrics-server dangerous, because then every cell is empty, nothing says why, and "does anything leak" goes unanswered for the night. It is asserted once at startup, and the assertion has to straddle two failures that do not look alike: a missing metrics-server exits non-zero, while a label matching no pod exits **zero** and writes "No resources found" to stderr, so the probe deliberately keeps stderr out of the value it tests — folding it in with `2>&1` makes the no-pod case read as a healthy sample, which is the same false pass the check exists to prevent. Five new `selftest.sh` checks guard the ones that are mechanically checkable, each verified by injecting the defect it names and watching it fail, and each reading a comment-stripped copy of the source — because the first version of the distroless check was failed by soak.sh's own comment warning against the thing it forbids. ([#1042](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1042))
- **A guardrail halt is now its own event on the attach stream instead of a turn's error.** A cost ceiling crossing or a watchdog trip arrived as a `turn-error` of kind `cost_ceiling` / `watchdog`, which put a session-level fact in a turn-level frame — a halt means *every* turn from here is refused until an operator resets it, it outlives the turn it interrupted, and at a turn boundary it interrupts nothing at all. Modelling it as a turn outcome forced a choice between two wrong things: emit the trip as the turn's terminal frame and the turn's real outcome goes unreported, or emit both and one turn appears to end twice. Protocol **1.13.0** adds `guardrail-trip` (`guardrail`, `reason`, `halted_turn`) as a **non-terminal** frame, so it sits alongside whatever the turn does next rather than replacing it; it deliberately does not participate in `awaitTerminalBarrier`, and the event name is the one [#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643)'s durable eventlog row already uses, because one occurrence should not have two names depending on whether it is read live or replayed. `halted_turn` is the field the event exists for and it is a **consumer obligation**, not a decoration: `true` means the turn was cut and exactly one `canceled` turn-error follows, which the client should suppress — one-shot, and dropped at the turn boundary, or a halt will swallow an operator's own Esc on a later turn; `false` means the trip landed at a boundary and the ordinary `turn-complete` follows. It is serialized even when false, so a consumer cannot infer it from absence and then silently stop suppressing. **This deletes half of [#818](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/818)** and the reason is worth stating: #818 removed a contentless `⚠ canceled` block from under a meaningful warning by having the producer not emit the cancel, and a turn's terminal frame is not the producer's to withhold — a consumer reconciling turn state is owed it. So the decision moves to the consumer, where it is a rendering question, and `halted_turn` is what makes it answerable there. #818's other half survives untouched: the `markGuardrailHalt` / `consumeGuardrailHalt` marker still labels `gen_ai.agent.invocation.duration` with the guardrail rather than with `canceled`. One consequence is sharper than "the kinds become rare" and is easy to miss: a turn **refused** by an already-tripped guardrail emits no frame at all. `preflightCostCeiling` and `preflightWatchdog` return a fresh iterator that records the invocation and yields the error, short-circuiting above the only `emit(EventTurnError, …)` site in the tree, so the refusal reaches the caller as a returned error and as `error.type` on the metric and never as SSE. `cost_ceiling` and `watchdog` therefore leave the wire entirely at 1.13.0; the kinds remain defined, because `ClassifyTurnError` still produces them for the returned error and for anything that surfaces one by another route. Conformance fixture `guardrail-trip-boundary-v1.13.0.json` pins the boundary shape, `internal/attachclient` decodes the frame, and the reference docs say in three places that a consumer MUST stop inferring a halt from the turn-error. **The bundled client ships with it in the same commit, because here that is not a convenience.** `core-tui` goes **v0.24.1 → v0.25.0** ([core-tui#313](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/pull/313)) and `internal/coretuiremote` projects the new frame through `translateTypedFrame`, which is what makes `core-agent-tui` paint a `⚠ guardrail halted · watchdog` block and swallow the cancel underneath it. Unlike `pause` and `wake`, whose older-client story was silence, an un-bumped client against a 1.13.0 daemon *regresses* — it loses the halt and gains the bare cancel — and there is no producer-side mitigation, because the suppression that used to hide the cancel is precisely what this change removes. The v0.25.0 release notes say the same thing from the host author's side. ([#891](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/891))
- **A daemon whose every turn is failing no longer looks exactly like a daemon with nothing to do.** `runner.WakeLoop` logged each turn error and went straight back to blocking — right for liveness, and the reason a persistent fault was invisible from outside the process: a running pod, a green readiness probe, and no work getting done. Two things change. The loop now counts *consecutive failing turns* and holds off for a capped, doubling interval before driving another, so a revoked binding or an exhausted quota costs at most a dozen turns an hour instead of one per arriving event, at whatever rate the cluster produces them. And `GET /healthz` grows a second check, `wake_loops`, which is what makes the condition externally visible at all — [#946](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/946) shipped the endpoint with one subsystem on it, and this is the one the endpoint's own rationale ("strictly more informative than the `tcpSocket` probe recipes fall back to") was pointing at. Four decisions are load-bearing and each has a test that fails without it. **The count is per TURN, not per error.** `OnTurnError` fires once per *yielded* error and one turn can yield several, so a counter driven off the callback would read one bad turn as three and back off at triple rate; the loop latches a single kind for the turn and observes once. **The first failure is free** — a blip must not add latency to the operator's next message — and only a repeat earns the rate limit, which is also why any clean turn resets both the interval and the state rather than decaying it. **Three error kinds do not count**: `canceled`, `watchdog` and `cost_ceiling` are the system obeying somebody, and counting them would make a daemon report itself unhealthy for working correctly and — worse — rate-limit the very turn that runs after an operator clears a halt. They do not count as *recoveries* either, which is the same distinction read the other way: a turn that was stopped attempted nothing, so it cannot be evidence the fault is gone, and treating it as a clean turn would let an operator pressing stop during a real outage silently reset the count and turn the gate green at the moment it most needs to stay red. **And the aggregate is one check, red only when every live loop has failed three turns in a row.** Not one turn: [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935)'s live batch measured fourteen provider 429s in ninety minutes, and descheduling a pod for one of those would make the readiness gate a signal operators learn to ignore. The predicate is the failure count rather than the reported state, so a probe landing inside a retry — where the loop reads `working` — does not see a broken session as recovered. One check per session would grow the `checks` map without bound under a kubelet re-probing on a fixed period, and `HealthCheck.Name` is a compile-time constant precisely because the endpoint is unauthenticated and a session id is not the caller's business; the per-session detail — which sessions, how many failures, which kind, since when — rides the error into the daemon log, which is already where the handler writes it. A *partial* outage stays green on purpose: readiness is a routing decision, and one session with revoked RBAC must not pull a pod serving forty-nine healthy ones out of its Service. The issue's third scope item is dropped rather than implemented. "Decide poison-event policy: drop after N attempts, or park the session" assumes a redelivery path, and there is none — `drainInboxFull` *consumes*, so a message that killed the turn processing it is already gone and cannot be re-delivered; a poison event is a one-turn failure that the consecutive counter then treats like any other. Its second half — "stop after a bound" — is deliberately **not** implemented as a stop, and the two available shapes are both worse than the fault. Returning closes the inbox ([#566](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/566)'s `CloseInbox` on the way out), so every later inject fails with `ErrInboxClosed` — including the operator's message telling the session what to do about the failure, which means the loop would destroy the only channel through which it could be fixed. Parking while refusing to drive turns is quieter and no better: the inbox is bounded at 256 and drops the **oldest** message when full, so a producer pointed at a stopped loop silently loses its earliest signals. That is precisely the [#1040](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1040) harm, and [#1040](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1040) is safe to fence only because a guardrail halt has a reset verb that releases it; a loop that stopped itself has none. So the bound is expressed as a **rate** — at most one failing turn per five minutes, indefinitely, with the condition on `/healthz` the whole time — and an operator who wants a real stop already has the verbs: pause the session, or delete it. One consequence lands outside Go. The bundled recipes had liveness and readiness both on `httpGet /healthz`, on the argument that a subsystem still degraded after several readiness failures is a restart candidate. That argument does not survive this check: the faults `wake_loops` reports are the ones a restart cannot fix, so sharing the definition would convert a daemon that is still up, still accepting injects and still saying what is wrong into a restart loop that drops the operator's context every sixty seconds. `gke-platform-agent`, `gke-troubleshoot-agent` and `gke-deploy` move liveness back to `tcpSocket`, each with the reasoning at the probe. ([#978](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/978))
- **A Vertex 429 no longer kills a subagent delegation, and the reason the Anthropic path did not need the same fix is now a test rather than an assumption.** [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) gated itself on evidence — *"one occurrence; the next one is the second data point, and the predicate should not be written before it exists"* — so the 38-run GKE drill archive was scanned for structured provider errors (`sse == "turn-error"` and ADK `event.ErrorCode`, not raw text, after a first pass matching prose reported 503 in 38 of 38 runs and was thrown away). **Four runs lost a subagent delegation to a 429 `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, three of them inside a single 20-run batch.** The empty-`Details` 400 from [#898](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/898) is still 1 of 38 and stays unwritten. What makes this worth fixing is not the failure rate but what the failure looks like: **3 of the 4 children died on their very first model call**, having recorded a plan and read nothing, and the runs *still scored*, because the parent quietly did the cluster work itself. The visible symptom is not a dead run, it is a run that silently loses the context isolation delegation exists to provide at full parent-context cost — and in one of the four (`20260912T104607Z-c`) the operator was never told, the archive's first `delegation-undisclosed`, which retires the narrow-corpus caveat [#1030](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1030) recorded. The issue's own objection — *"a retry policy that fires in exactly that window adds load precisely when the provider is shedding it"* — is answered from the archive rather than argued: in **4 of 4** cases the parent's next model-backed call, same session, same model, 3 to 4 seconds later, succeeded. These are momentary rejections inside a window the provider is otherwise serving. The honest counter-evidence shapes the guard rather than being omitted: two of the four arrived 8 minutes apart at the tail of a sustained 84-minute batch, so cumulative pressure is real and the data supports retry-**once**-with-backoff, not retry-always. So `models.RetryPolicy` retries a streaming call once, and the cooldown is the load bound — **one extra request per window for the whole process**, which is why the policy is package-level rather than a field on the model wrapper: Vertex quota is enforced per project and region, and a daemon running a parent plus three subagents through four `Model()` calls must not be able to fire four retries into one shed. Under a genuine shed the first call retries and every later one degrades to plain pass-through. Three invariants are load-bearing and each has a test that fails without it. It retries **only when no usable content reached the caller** — once content has been yielded a replay would duplicate the turn, so from that point the stream is pass-through and a late error surfaces unchanged; the buffer-and-drop-partials shape is lifted from the cache-eviction wrapper, which solved the same streaming problem for a different trigger. A **non-transient error sitting in the buffer suppresses the retry**, because a retry discards the buffer, and retrying past a real error would tell the caller about the rate limit and never about the malformed request that actually killed the call. And the predicate is **narrow on purpose**: 429/`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` and 503/`UNAVAILABLE`, typed via `genai.APIError` first with a substring fallback that pairs each code with its status word — the discriminator convention `vertexcache.IsCacheGone` established, and not optional in a drill that reads live cluster state, where a pod named `svc-unavailable-7d9` and a model quoting "429 failed probes" are both in the corpus. `attach.ClassifyTurnError` also has a `Retryable` bit and is deliberately **not** reused: it runs on a turn that has already failed, where breadth is cheap, and it matches a bare `unavailable` substring; this one runs on an in-flight request where a false positive re-sends something the provider refused on purpose. Both sites now say so. **It has since been verified on a cluster**, which [#1037](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1037) shipped without doing and its own review said was the only thing that would settle it: 21 runs, `dev/uat/gke-drill/runs/2026-09-13-std-simian-test-bc-20run-post935.md`. Do not quote the rate — 4 of 38 runs losing a delegation against 1 of 21 is Fisher's exact **p = 0.65**, and a batch able to resolve that would need to be several times larger. The evidence is mechanical and it comes from the daemon log, which is where `RetryPolicy` writes and which no batch had read before: **fourteen Vertex 429s reached the policy in ninety minutes, thirteen were retried, and not one of those thirteen went on to kill anything** — at $0.0957/run against $0.0959 for the pre-fix batch the day before, so the added requests do not show up in the bill. That statement does not depend on the two batches having faced comparable quota pressure, which visibly they did not. The single surviving failure is the single **suppressed** retry, and the suppression is this policy's own: the parent's 429 at 10:14:32 consumed the cooldown window, the subagent's 429 six seconds later was refused, and its `spawn_agent` failure is stamped 745 microseconds after the "NOT retried" line. Process-wide scoping is still right for exactly the reason given above; what it misses is that **429s are correlated by construction** — a shed is the condition under which several concurrent callers are rejected within seconds — so one rescue per 30s is spent on whoever arrives first and abandons the rest of the burst, which is the traffic shape the feature was built for. [#1039](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1039) carries the recommendation (a token bucket, burst 3, refill one per 30s) together with a second finding from the same log — ten of the thirteen retries logged no outcome at all, because `Wrap` returns bare on the consumer-stop path and skips the recovery line the new provider docs tell operators to look for. Neither is changed here: n = 1 for the suppression, and this project has already been bitten once by acting on a three-run reading. The cross-provider half of the scope inverted on contact with the evidence. The premise was that `pkg/models/anthropic` had no retry and no backoff anywhere, which is true of this repo and false of the stack: **anthropic-sdk-go already retries twice by default** on 408, 409, 429 and every 5xx including 529, honouring `retry-after` — so its backoff is the server's own number rather than a constant we picked. Wrapping it would have multiplied to six requests for one turn and fired only after the SDK had already backed off twice, which is the strongest available evidence the rejection is *not* momentary. The asymmetry [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) set out to close is between the two SDKs, not between our two adapters, and `google.golang.org/genai` has no retry of any kind. So that adapter is left alone — but the claim is pinned down by three tests driving the real SDK against an `httptest` server that 429s, 529s, and refuses three times in a row, so if anyone ever passes `option.WithMaxRetries(0)` the comment stops being true and CI says so. ([#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935))
- **The drill's evidence sheet now prints what a delegation *did*, and the rubric finding its silence produced has been withdrawn.** [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014) — a parent re-issuing reads its subagent had already made — was found by hand, in a raw transcript, *after* seven sheets had been signed 6/6. Every fact it rests on was already in those sheets: the child's reads were in G1, the parent's reads were in G1, and nothing ever printed them next to each other. `score.py` now emits a `## Delegation` section per `spawn_agent`, giving the child's name and the seq it returned at, the `status`/`stop_reason` the result carried, whether that result carried [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014)'s `calls` provenance at all, what the child read, how many of the parent's **post-handoff** reads repeat one of those reads at the same fidelity, and whether the parent's answer says out loud that it delegated. It is **explicitly not a box**: `SCORECARD.md` has six, this is none of them, and nothing here moves a score — the same refusal `score.py` already makes for G1, G2, G3 and G6, for the same reason. It reports a clean delegation exactly as loudly as a repeated one, because a section that only speaks up when it has a complaint teaches the reader to treat its silence as a pass, and silence is precisely what hid [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014) through seven sheets. Three distinctions are load-bearing and each has an assertion that fails without it. An **escalation is not a repeat** — a table-format read carries no `spec`, so going back for one in YAML fetches a fidelity the first read did not have; the existing `compare_reads` already drew that line for G6 and this section reuses it rather than growing a second opinion. A read the parent made **before** it delegated is not a post-handoff repeat, and no fixture had a parent read on that side of the boundary until now. And the child's own registered name is **deliberately absent** from the disclosure words: the child is called `cluster`, a word in nearly every sentence a GKE answer contains, so searching for it would mark every run disclosed — the [#996](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/996)–[#1000](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1000) rig defect exactly, where a name asserted on both sides of a check made the check untestable. `dev/trajectory` excludes it for the same reason and the two must agree. The cost is that a parent disclosing in other words reads as undisclosed, so the sheet says so at the point of use rather than leaving it to be rediscovered. A new `testdata/provenance-run` fixture carries the post-[#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014) shape — a result with `calls`, a truncation the parent is told about, an answer that discloses, and a pre-handoff parent read — because without it half the section would be code no fixture runs; `dryrun.sh` asserts the section end to end, which is the only place the `subagents.json` join gets tested rather than handed in pre-joined. Finally, `runs/REVIEW-GUIDE.md`'s open question "is G2 blind to under-claiming?" has been narrowed to what actually survives. The structural argument stands: an agent that said "I cannot verify" about everything would pass G2 on all seven runs. The one concrete instance put forward for it does not — the 2026-09-11 scenario A run whose `cluster` subagent died of a Vertex 429 *did* disclose, opening its answer with "(Note: Diagnostic subagent delegation failed due to API rate limiting…)", and the claim was withdrawn in that run's sheet by [#1030](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1030). Across the fifteen-run archive `dev/trajectory` counts one `delegation-failed`, one `delegation-disclosed` and zero `delegation-undisclosed` — and since the latter two fire only on a delegation that *failed*, what the corpus establishes is narrow and now says so: one failed delegation, disclosed, and nothing at all about the fourteen that succeeded. ([#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014))
- **A delegation now hands back what the subagent *did*, not only what it said, which is the half of [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014) the parent was re-reading to reconstruct.** Both doors — `spawn_agent { wait: true }` and a declarative subagent reached as a named tool — return `calls`: every tool call the child made, in order, with its arguments and its error if it failed, alongside a `calls_truncated` count and a `calls_note` line. The measurement is [#1030](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1030)'s, read on the scenario that actually isolates the behaviour: the drill's OOMKill follow-up asks for a value the subagent already established **and** for the read behind it, and before this change 4 of 6 runs re-issued one of the child's reads, 66% of the bytes the parent pulled back after the handoff. Across 11 runs afterwards it is **0 of 11, and zero bytes** — an exact binomial p of 5.6 × 10⁻⁶. Resist pooling that across the drill: the RBAC scenario's follow-up asks whether the workload is healthy *now*, which is a value the child's report never carried and no amount of provenance can supply, and it repeats at the same rate before and after because it should ([#1034](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1034)). The parent was not misbehaving. It hands back prose, prose cannot be cited, and a parent graded on grounding its claims has exactly one way to obtain a citation: issue the read itself. So the fix is not a bigger payload or a better-compressed one — it is **provenance**, which the runtime already observes going past as function-call events. A recorded call runs about a hundred bytes against the ~3,120 the average repeated read cost, and isolation survives untouched, because a record of what was *run* is not a copy of what came *back*. Three properties are load-bearing and each has a test that fails without it. The list is **observed, never self-reported** — built from the child's event stream, so a subagent can neither omit nor invent an entry, and the parent is not asked to trust a second claim in order to check the first. The driver's own control tools are **excluded**, the return tool above all: its argument already *is* the result's `output`, and recording it would ship the same prose twice in one payload. And a run that **died** still carries the calls it made before dying — the 2026-09-11 run lost its delegation to a 429 after one call, and that call was the only citable thing about it. `calls_note` is prose deliberately, applying the lesson [#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710) paid for: a well-formed field two levels down does not change what a language model does, so the note sits next to the array and says in language that these may be cited, and that re-running one is warranted only to learn whether something has *since changed*. Re-reading for freshness is the job; re-reading to manufacture a citation you already hold is paying twice for one fact, and only the second is what this fixes — a distinction `delegation-repeated-read` cannot draw, which is why the corpus-wide rate is the wrong number to quote and [#1034](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1034) exists. Both doors are covered for the reason [#758](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/758) had to gate twice — a declarative subagent has two of them, and a fix on one is a fix a recipe can miss by wiring the other. A child that called no tools serializes exactly as it did before. ([#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014))
- **The deployable recipes move onto the 2.9.0 GA, and `examples/gke-deploy` finally gets the health probe it was made to wait for.** `gke-platform-agent` and `gke-troubleshoot-agent` were pinned to `2.9.0-dev.6`, the pre-release the drill ran against; `gke-deploy` and `cloud-run-deploy` were on the 2.8.0 GA under comments saying they track the newest one. All four now pin `2.9.0`. Nothing in CI forced this — `cut-ga-tag.sh` folds a pre-release section into the GA entry but leaves a trailer behind, and the pin gate reads that trailer, so a `-dev.6` pin stays valid forever. It is wrong anyway: the recipes are what an operator copies, and copying a pre-release when its GA exists is a worse deploy for no reason. `examples/gke-deploy` also moves both probes from `tcpSocket` to `httpGet /healthz`. [#993](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/993) deliberately left it behind — it pinned a GA, and trading a stability promise for a better probe is the wrong way round — with a note saying to collect the fix at the next GA in the same change that moves the pin. This is that change. A bare TCP connect passes as soon as `:7777` accepts, including when the session store behind it has stopped answering; `/healthz` reports that. Two deploys still need `tcpSocket` back, and the manifest says so where the probe is: rolling back to a pre-2.9 daemon, whose images have no `/healthz` and will 401 every probe, and setting `attach.client_ca`, whose handshake demands a client certificate kubelet does not present. Separately, `gke-platform-agent`'s two overlays were still pinning the `v2` content image after [#1010](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1010) moved `prereqs.sh` to `v3`; `set-up-demo.sh` substitutes that tag at apply time so no scripted deploy was affected, but a plain `kubectl apply -k` — which the README invites — would have run the v3 recipe against v2 content. ([#1025](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1025))
- **`dev/trajectory` grows its first measure, and it puts a rate on [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014): in 8 of 15 archived drill runs the parent re-issues a read its subagent already made.** The analyzer shipped in [#1028](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1028) could print what an agent did; it could not say anything about it. A `Measure` now observes a loaded trajectory and emits `Observation`s — kind, agent, step, one-line summary, evidence — which the CLI prints under each table and tallies by kind across `--all`. The first and only measure is `delegation`, reporting four kinds: `delegation-repeated-read` (10 occurrences across 8 of 15 runs), `delegation-failed` (1), `delegation-disclosed` (1) and `delegation-undisclosed` (0). **Observations carry no severity, no score and no pass/fail**, for the reason the package header already gives — the rubric in `SCORECARD.md` is still moving, and a measure that only ever speaks when something is wrong is indistinguishable, on a clean corpus, from a measure that is broken. So where the distinction is the point, both sides are reported: `delegation-disclosed` fires as loudly as `delegation-undisclosed`. The measure set is short on purpose and every entry must fire on the real archive, which is what kept it to one: [#967](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/967)'s loop rate would report zero fifteen times, and it waits for the [#966](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/966) corpus. The corpus overruled the design three separate times and each is recorded in `doc.go`, because the *near misses* are the finding here.
