A `delegation-result-dropped` measure was planned around [#641](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/641) ("`wait:true` returns a summary, not findings") — [#641](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/641) is closed and the archive confirms the fix, with `spawn_agent`'s `output` equal to the child's `return_result` `result` byte-for-byte in all 14 healthy runs, so the measure would have reported a defect that no longer exists. The repeated-read signature originally matched arguments exactly, found 5 repeats in 5 runs, and **missed the case that motivated it**: the child read a resource as YAML and the parent re-read it unformatted, which exact matching scores as two different reads and a human scores as one; ignoring arguments that only change rendering moved it to 10 across 8, so that normalization is load-bearing rather than cosmetic. And the disclosure check deliberately does *not* count the child's own registered name as evidence of disclosure — the child is called `cluster`, a word in nearly every answer this drill produces, and admitting it would mark every run disclosed and make the measure a rubber stamp. That is the [#996](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/996)–[#1000](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1000) rig defect in miniature, a name asserted on both sides of a check is untested, and a dedicated test now holds the exclusion in place. The cost is the acceptable direction of error: a parent that discloses without using any of the words reads as undisclosed, which costs one glance at the quoted answer and, for something that is not a gate, is the right way to be wrong. Still not a gate, still no CI wiring, still exit 0 whatever it finds. Narrows [#994](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/994).
- **A recorded run can now be read as what the agent DID, in order, instead of only as how its answer scored.** New `dev/trajectory` (library) and `dev/trajectory/cmd/trajectory` (CLI) load a GKE-drill run directory — `meta.json`, `transcript.jsonl`, `subagents.json` — and print the ordered tool calls of the parent and every subagent, interleaved by sequence, with each call's result joined on, its status, and how long it took. The six rubric boxes grade an answer and [#652](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/652)'s eval runner grades effects on the world; neither can see a trajectory, and trajectories fail on their own: [#711](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/711) reached a correct end state in 44 turns and $1.33 against $0.26 for the comparable run, and the `list_agents` runaway behind the [#623](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/623)–[#627](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/627) guardrails had a fine end state too. Go turned out to be the cheap substrate rather than the expensive one: the `event` object the drill records is a marshalled `google.golang.org/adk/session.Event`, the same type `pkg/eventlog` persists, and all fifteen archived runs decode into it strictly, so the analyzer gets typed `FunctionCall.Args`, `FunctionResponse.Response` and `UsageMetadata` where `score.py` hand-walks the same dicts. Reading the whole archive as step tables showed three things the evidence sheets do not: the 2026-09-11 run that scored 6/6 with a dead `cluster` subagent makes **6 steps with 1 in the child**, against 11–12 steps with 9–10 in the child for every other scenario A, so the failed delegation is a column rather than a hand-written note; [#1014](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1014)'s "a parent cannot cite its subagent's reads, so it re-issues them" is visible as row order, the child returning its analysis and the parent, right after the handoff, re-reading what it had already read; and no run in the archive contains a loop or a redundant call *within one agent*, which is why the first measure is about delegation shape and not about redundancy — such a measure would report zero across the entire corpus and would therefore be untested. **This ships as an instrument and not as a gate**: it renders no verdict, has no CI wiring and no threshold, and a non-zero exit means only that the tool itself failed, following `dev/lookout-pin-check`'s convention for the same reason. [#790](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/790) and [#844](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/844) spent 6,146 lines on a drift detector that detected correctly and changed nothing because attention, not detection, was the bottleneck. The one rule this must not drift on is what counts as a failed tool call, so `responseStatus` mirrors `score.py`'s three-way `ok`/`error`/`suspect` rule exactly — structural signals decide `error`, a 200-character prose scan only raises `suspect` — and the mirroring is checked rather than asserted, against the tool-call tally `score.py` itself wrote into each run's `evidence.md`: fifteen runs, five counts each, zero disagreements. Getting there turned up a genuine trap worth recording. A 200-character window makes the **JSON encoding part of the rule**, and Go's differs from Python's: Python writes `", "` and `": "` where Go writes `","` and `":"`, so Go's window reaches two characters deeper per field. That is not a rounding difference — it is the whole disagreement on this corpus. One step's payload is a pod listing whose `STATUS` column reads `Error` about 180 characters in, inside Go's window and past the end of Python's, and with compact separators the two tools disagree about whether that call failed. `pyDumps` reproduces CPython's `json.dumps` (separators, key order, `\uXXXX` escaping, float repr) so the cut lands in the same place, verified byte-for-byte against CPython 3.12 on 23 cases. Narrows [#994](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/994).

#### Bug or Regression
- **A denied edit no longer blocks every other edit to the same file for the rest of the turn.** The turn-refusal memory keyed file-tool calls on their path alone, so one denied edit made a different edit to that file read as "an identical request". A denial is now keyed on the call's full arguments. A person who denies one edit in ask mode is asked about a different one. ([#1343](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1343))
- **A model LiteLLM marks deprecated keeps its price for 90 days instead of losing it that day.**
  - **Why it matters:** core-models' table generator used to drop a model's rows on LiteLLM's `deprecation_date`, even while the model still answered. Since #1327, a model with no price under a cost ceiling is refused at startup, so a dropped row would have stopped every such deployment from starting.
  - **What it would have hit:** on 2026-10-20 the old rule would have dropped the three Gemini 2.5 models, `gemini-2.5-flash`, `-flash-lite` and `-pro`: their bare, `gemini/` and `vertex/` rows and their context windows. A live probe found all three still answering. (core-models' own report first said 13; it counted deprecation dates on rows the rule never gates.)
  - **The fix:** core-models v0.13.5 keeps a deprecated model's rows (bare, backend-qualified and context window) for 90 days past the date. A model a probe confirms gone is still dropped early by name.
  - **Also in v0.13.5:** two new rows (`openai/gpt-5.5-cyber-preview`, `vertex-maas/deepseek-ai/deepseek-v4.1-flash-maas`). No rate moved and nothing was removed.
- **Explicit context caching on Gemini 4 Argon gives up once instead of retrying for eight minutes.** Vertex AI doesn't support explicit context caches for `gemini-4-argon` and rejects them with a 400. The cache manager used to try six times over at least eight minutes (it retries only on new turns), logging `Caches.Create failed (attempt N of 6; …)` each time and then `failed 6 times (giving up…)`. core-models v0.13.4 treats it like a prompt below the cache minimum: one log line, then requests run uncached. The cache manager is shared by the whole process, so on a multi-session daemon every session runs uncached for the life of the process. Argon's implicit cache hits are still billed at the cache-read rate. ([#1335](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1335))
- **A daily price refresh no longer costs a backend its own rates.** The refresh stored LiteLLM's keys verbatim (`vertex_ai/claude-…`), and the refreshed layer outranks the built-in table. So after the first refresh, a model priced per backend fell back to its bare first-party row. Claude Sonnet 4.5 on Vertex AI billed a turn of 300K tokens in and 4K out at $0.96 instead of its long-context $1.89. For any model priced per backend, that undid the long-context fix below in every deployment running the default refresh. core-models v0.13.3 fixes both halves:
  - A refresh now adds backend-qualified rows (`anthropic-vertex/`, `vertex/`, `gemini/`, `vertex-maas/`, `openai/`, `xai/`) next to LiteLLM's own keys, using the same mapping as its table generator. The refresh's model count in the startup line goes up accordingly.
  - A cache an older refresh wrote heals without the operator doing anything. It is re-qualified as it loads, so the right rates apply even offline or under `--no-pricing-refresh`. The next refresh also counts it as stale whatever its age, and fetches in full without its cached ETag; `/pricing refresh` now always fetches in full.
  - One limit: an old cache's `openai/` rows aren't re-derived until that fetch, but their bare rows carry the same rates.
- **A turn over a model's long-context threshold is billed at the long-context rate.** Until now every model had one flat rate, so cost reporting and the `max_*_cost_usd` ceilings under-counted long prompts: Claude Haiku 5.5 by up to 5x above 100K prompt tokens (the gap 3.0.0-dev.1 called out), Gemini 2.5 Pro and 3.1 Pro by about 2x above 200K, Claude Sonnet 4.5 on Vertex AI above 200K. `usage.Pricing` carries the model's `LongContext` row, and its cost methods share core-models' arithmetic: a prompt (uncached + cache reads + cache writes) over the threshold bills every bucket and the output at the long-context rates. Below the threshold nothing moves. An old-vs-new price dump over 168 model ids, 8 backends and 3 usage shapes changed no price except these, and lost none. ([#1328](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1328))
- **A provider profile's declared `context_window` now sizes compaction.** core-models v0.8.0 lets a profile model state the window its server actually serves (`"context_window": 65536` on a `models[]` entry — for vLLM, its `--max-model-len`). Compaction and the boot `compaction: fires at …` line now use that ahead of the built-in table, so a self-hosted model capped below its published window compacts before the server rejects the request, and an uncatalogued profile model no longer falls back to the assumed 128,000 tokens when its profile says otherwise. Only profiles the process routes to count — `model.provider`, then each `subagents[].model.provider` — so a profile that is declared but unused never resizes another provider's session of the same model id. The remote attach TUI still shows the catalog window for such a model; the daemon does not send its window over attach yet. ([#1320](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1320))
- **Cost ceilings check a request's input cost before sending it, and a model nobody prices is refused where it is configured.** Ceilings used to be checked only after each model call, so one request that was over budget by itself was always sent: a turn was once cut at $1.19 against a $0.25 bound. The per-turn ceiling and a subagent's `budgets.max_cost_usd` now estimate each request's input cost (the last measured input plus tool results since, or the serialized request; a cached prefix at the cache-read rate) and refuse it with the existing per-turn trip when that alone would reach the bound. A request is priced on the backend that bills it, and a subagent's request from its own context, under the parent's ceiling and under a subagent's own budget alike. Side calls (summarizer, title, `/btw`, approver, agentic subtasks, MCP digests) are not screened, an estimate is kept only for a request that produced usage, and a turn's check ends with its turn, so a background subagent still running afterwards is not screened by it. Output tokens can't be known up front, so the bound is still a floor. A ceiling over a model with no price could never trip, since it counted every call as $0. That is now a config error at startup on every provider: the main model, declared subagents' models, the background small tier, and any declared subagent's `budgets.max_cost_usd`. `/model` and background spawns refuse such a model the same way, and so does an attach client's spawn that passes its own `max_cost_usd`. The default background budget, and one the parent's model picks, only warn, once per model. The unattended default session ceiling only warns. The remedy named in each message: set `model.pricing` for the model (zero rates are fine there for a self-hosted model you don't pay per token for), declare real `models[].rates` in its provider profile, or drop the ceiling or budget. A model that still reaches a ceiling at runtime is skipped with one warning and never counts toward the three-trips halt. Contributed by @mikemikimike. ([#1327](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1327))
- **Claude Sonnet 4.5 sessions compact against a 200K context window again, not 1M.** LiteLLM's catalog moved `claude-sonnet-4-5` (and its dated id) back to 200,000, and the built-in table follows it, so a Sonnet 4.5 session compacts before reaching the window the API actually serves without the long-context opt-in, which core-agent never sends. `-1m` ids still get 1M. ([#1316](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1316))
- **The scion image reports its real version again, and release pages print an install command that works.** The release-notes footer every GitHub Release carries told users to run `go install github.com/go-steer/core-agent/cmd/core-agent@TAG`, a path with no major suffix that can never resolve a v2+ tag; it now names `/v3`, as do the site's API package table and the gke-parallel-triage README, which had the same suffix-less paths. `extras/scion/Dockerfile` injected the version, commit and date with `-X github.com/go-steer/core-agent/internal/version.*`, a path without the `/v2` suffix the module gained in #327. Go silently ignores `-X` for a symbol that does not exist, so since then the image's `core-agent --version` has shown the in-source fallback rather than the tag it was built from. The flags now name the `/v3` path, and `verify-version-fallback` now fails when an `-X` flag or a live install path names a different module than `go.mod`. ([#1312](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1312))
- **A rejected `spawn_agent` now says what would have worked.** An unknown subagent name used to come back as just `unknown predefined subagent: "k8s"`, and an unknown tool as just `unknown tool: "bash"`. A parent with no cluster access of its own, which is a normal shape for `--no-builtin-tools`, was seen guessing its way through about 27 names, one rejected spawn each, while the watchdog raised four warnings. The errors now list the names that do resolve and the tools a subagent can be granted, or say plainly that there are none: inline subagents only, or no tools to grant. The refusal for an inline subagent when those are disabled no longer sends the model to "a configured subagent" that does not exist. On a build with nothing to grant, `spawn_agent`'s `tools` parameter now says so instead of staying silent. The `extras` parameter no longer offers `kubectl_get` as an example, because most builds cannot grant it. A deployment with no configured subagents keeps its tool description and `agent` parameter exactly as before. In three live no-access runs per side, the worst case went from 27 spawns and 4 watchdog warnings to 1 spawn and none. ([#1303](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1303))
- **After a session compacts, the model sees the user's message again.** Once a session held a compaction or checkpoint summary, every later turn sent the model the summary and `[End of summary. The actual user message follows below.]`, with nothing after it. The operator's message was missing, and so were the turn's own tool calls and results. A tool turn's follow-up request therefore didn't show the model the response it was waiting for, and a model in that state tends to call the same tool again until the watchdog stops it. The post-summary view of the session was computed once, when the turn started, and everything the turn appended after that (the user message, each call, each result) went to the real session behind it. The view is now recomputed from the real session every time a request is built. This affected the in-memory session store and the durable `--session-db` eventlog alike. ([#1292](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1292))
- **A wake on a fresh session with nothing queued no longer fails on Claude.** `POST /wake` on a new session with an empty inbox ran a turn with no conversation in it. Anthropic answered `400 messages: at least one message is required`, and the turn ended in a `turn-error`. Gemini was spared only because ADK v1's Gemini model quietly added a user turn of its own. ADK v2 dropped that, so the same wake would have started failing on Gemini too (`400 INVALID_ARGUMENT: at least one contents field is required`, seen live before this fix). core-agent now sends that user turn itself, with ADK v1's wording ("Handle the requests as specified in the System Instruction."), for every provider, whenever a request would otherwise reach the model empty. A wake on a session that already has history is unchanged. ([#1291](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1291))
- **Vertex's bare `400 INVALID_ARGUMENT` now gets one retry when the session has already been served.** The error has no details and the message `Request contains an invalid argument.` It ended a drill turn on 2026-10-06, straight after a successful turn on the same session, config and model. That is the shape #898 recorded, where the session kept working afterwards. It was classed `config_error`, so the transient retry never ran and the operator got no answer. It is now retried once, under the shared retry budget, only when an earlier model call in the same agent session has succeeded. A subagent needs a success of its own, and internal side calls (including an MCP digest and an agentic tool's subtask) never qualify. A 400 on a session's first call, a 400 that names a field or carries details, and any other status still fail exactly as before. The retry logs, stamps and prefixes the same way a 429 retry does, so box A2 counts it on both sides. A 400 that persists reads `provider retry persisted: …`, one that finds the budget spent reads `provider retry skipped, budget spent: …`, and both are still `config_error`/`400`. The Anthropic adapter is unchanged. Design: `docs/retry-bare-invalid-argument-design.md` ([#1247](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1247)).
- **A per-turn guardrail cut and a turn error now stay in the session, so a late attach or a replay shows them.** Both used to reach only the daemon log and whichever clients were attached at that moment. Replaying a session with `GET …/events?since=0` showed a turn that just stopped. The 2026-10-06 fault batch logged 7 cost-ceiling cuts and 7 turn errors and replayed 0 of each, which failed box A2. Each one now appends an event-log row, written at the same moment as the log line: `guardrail-turn-trip` (`Author=agent/guardrail-turn-trip`) for a trip that left the session running, the existing `guardrail-trip` halt row for one that halted it, and `turn-error` (`Author=agent/turn-error`) for every turn error, including a turn refused because the session is halted. The `turn-error` row carries the frame's `kind`/`code`/`message`/`retryable`/`hint`, plus `prompt_id` and `cut_by`, the guardrail whose cut caused it. The rows replay as ordinary `agent` frames. They carry no content, so the model never sees them, and compaction, usage and auto-continue skip them. A per-turn row can't be restored as a halt after a restart. Attach protocol **1.19.0**: the typed `guardrail-trip` and `turn-error` frames gain an optional `event_id` naming the row, so an attached client counts each failure once. `core-agent-tui` renders the replayed rows and shows each failure once. `dev/uat/gke-drill/a2_count.py` counts the rows, and an event that appears in both a live capture and a replay counts once. It takes `--since`/`--until` and drops log lines and transcript events outside that window on both sides, so a replay's older history can't cover a log-only failure. The new `replay_sessions.sh` saves the replay of every session the hub touched in a window, records the window, and prints the matching `a2_count.py` line. It exists because an incident can open more than one session and the drill captures one ([#1258](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1258)).
- **`examples/gke-platform-agent`: the content image builds again, and the recipe's default image carries the gated-apply content root.** #1105 added `COPY gated-apply/` to `deploy/content.Dockerfile` but not the matching staging step to `scripts/build-content-image.sh`, which builds from a `/tmp` copy of the recipe. Every content build since failed at that `COPY`, so the newest image anyone could have was `v4`, and a gated-apply deploy (`LEG=d1`/`d2`) on `v4` never starts: the read-only image volume has no mountpoint for the gated root's `plans` directory (`RunContainerError`). The script now stages `gated-apply/` and prunes local run state from it, `prereqs.sh` defaults to `CONTENT_TAG=v5` (built from the fix), and `TestEveryDockerfileCopyIsStagedByTheBuildScript` fails when a `COPY` source has no staging line. Found on the first live D1 run for box A3 ([#1042](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1042)).
- **`examples/gke-platform-agent`: switching back to `LEG=readonly` now removes the gated-apply patch grant, and proves the daemon cannot patch.** `kubectl apply -k` does not prune, so a read-only `set-up-demo.sh` run after a `LEG=d1` or `LEG=d2` one restored the read-only config and left the `gated-apply-gke-platform-agent` Role and RoleBinding in `TARGET_NS`: a standing grant to patch Deployments that nothing in the output mentioned, removed only by `teardown.sh`. A read-only deploy now deletes both once the read-only config is applied, using the same name lookup as `teardown.sh` (now one shared helper in `prereqs.sh`), says so, and runs `verify-gated-apply.sh denied` as a check that fails the deploy if the daemon can still patch. The d1/d2 legs are unchanged. ([#1249](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1249))
- **The soak grader would have called correct compaction behaviour a failure, and could have credited a compaction that never happened.** Both found on the live 2026-10-02 soak before it finished. First: compaction is marked when a turn *ends* and runs when the *next* turn starts, so a per-incident session that crosses the threshold during its one incident and then idles owes nothing — and `soak_verdict.py` scored every such session as "crossed and never compacted": FAIL. A crossing now counts against compaction only once the session's turn count goes flat for two samples and then moves again (one flat interval is a long tool call, not a turn ending); a session that crossed and idled is NOT EXERCISED. Second: a session whose last call was a side call reads that one prompt in `window` for as long as it idles — a persistent low reading the drop heuristic cannot tell from a compaction, observed as sessions ending the night at ~15K with zero compactions. When samples carry the `compactions` count the grader now decides on the count alone. Third, found on the first soak of the fixed image: a session that crosses and compacts within one sampling interval is never *seen* above the threshold, and the grader, keyed on a sampled crossing, called the clause NOT EXERCISED while the count said compacted; a non-zero count on a session the soak watched now counts as a compaction by itself. The README also records why A1's compaction clause runs with `--compaction-threshold=0.021`: on a 1M-token window the default 0.85 is out of reach of any eight-hour soak. ([#1199](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1199))
- **Lowering the compaction threshold now does something on every supported model.** An operator's `compaction.threshold` ranked *below* the built-in per-tier defaults (frontier 0.85, mid 0.65, small 0.35), so it governed only models the tier table did not recognise — none of the supported ones. `--compaction-threshold` writes that field and so does every task class, so all three were silently ignored: no error, no warning, no effect. Found when an overnight soak ran `--compaction-threshold=0.021` on `gemini-3.7-flash` and every session sailed past 21K tokens without compacting, the built-in 0.85 of a 1M window still deciding. The single threshold now replaces the built-in tier defaults for every tier; only an operator's own `threshold_by_tier` entries outrank it for their tier. Precedence: your `threshold_by_tier` entry → your `threshold` → the built-in tier default → 0.85. This also fixes a combination that reached neither value: config `threshold` plus `--task` on a recognised model used the built-in default. **Behaviour change:** a config that sets `compaction.threshold` now gets that threshold on recognised models, where before it silently got the tier default. ([#1226](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1226))
- **An `agentic_*` subtask or a digest-summarizer call no longer stands in for the conversation in the context gauge and the compaction check.** Both are model calls made mid-turn on the parent's behalf, and both were recorded as ordinary turns, so each became `Tracker.Last()` until the parent's next call landed. In that window the context gauge reported the subtask's prompt as the conversation's fill, the window was looked up for the subtask's model, a compaction check classified its tier from that model, and the #975 estimate of tool results appended since the parent's last call was thrown away. With a cheap subtask model that under-reports fill, which is the direction where compaction fails to fire before the provider rejects the request — and `gke-platform-agent` sets `agentic_wrap_llm: true`, so this was the overnight soak's own path. Both now record through `AppendSideUsage` (#1175's approver ledger): their cost still reaches the session totals and the cost ceilings, but `Last()`, the pending estimate and the per-turn usage frame keep describing the parent. No usage frame fires for a side call, so an attached client's session cost catches up on the parent's next model call — normally right after the tool returns, but if the turn ends first (an interrupt, a #975 cut, a provider error, or a cost-ceiling trip the subtask's own spend caused) it stays stale until the next turn or a re-attach. The ceilings themselves read the totals and are unaffected. Subagent billing has the same shape and is deliberately not changed here, because a long *background* subagent would then stop updating an attached client's cost until the parent next speaks; that trade-off is tracked in [#1203](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1203). ([#1191](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1191))
- **`core-tui` v0.28.0 → v0.28.1: the attach TUI no longer scrambles streamed text, and a permission prompt no longer crosses into another session on a switch.** A fix-only patch bump, no exported-API change. `core-agent-tui` could show a reply's streamed text out of order from the moment it attached, with no switch involved: core-tui ran two listeners on the event stream of an attached session, and consecutive chunks could reach the transcript in either order. It now runs exactly one ([core-tui#355](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/355)). On `/switch`, `/new` or `/attach <url> <sid>`, a prompt the session being left raised in the instant of the switch could still open over the new session, and the operator's answer then went nowhere, so the old session's tool call waited until `approval_timeout`. Since [#1183](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1183) each session has its own prompter, and core-tui now answers such a prompt with a deny sent to the session that raised it, the same answer a prompt already on screen at the switch gets. A prompt that arrives after the switch completes reaches no client until the operator switches back. Each switch also stops leaving a listener behind on the old session's prompter for the rest of the program ([core-tui#353](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/353)). `verify-coretui-guards` re-runs clean at 22 exported interfaces, 31 guards and 13 declines. ([#1189](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1189))
- **`core-agent-tui`'s permission prompts now follow the session on screen across `/switch`, `/new` and `/attach <url> <sid>`.** The prompt bridge was started once, at launch, on the startup session's `/perms/stream`, and no switch replaced it. After a switch, prompts from the session the operator was looking at never reached them, so the tool call blocked until `approval_timeout`, or for good when none is set, and prompts from the session they had left kept appearing over the new one. The deny-with-reason opt-in from #1182 had the same binding, so after `/attach` to a daemon on another protocol version the `r` key reflected the startup daemon. Each session now has its own prompter and bridge. The bridge runs while the TUI is attached to that session: it starts when the switch is applied and stops when the TUI detaches, and a prompt that was on screen at the switch still sends its deny to the session it came from. A switch that fails, or one cancelled with esc while it is still connecting, leaves the current session's prompts working. A prompt raised by a session after the operator leaves it now reaches no client until they switch back (the daemon replays it), or until `approval_timeout`; `approval_notify` treats it as a prompt nobody sees. ([#1183](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1183))
- **`stop_agent` now says when the subagent had already finished, instead of reporting its old status as if the stop had produced it.** The tool read the subagent's status back after stopping it. For a subagent that had already ended, that status was its own terminal one, such as `deferred`, and the tool's description promised `stopped`. In the #1116 self-development run the parent stopped a reviewer that had hit its max-cost bound twenty seconds earlier, got `deferred` back, and it and the operator both read that as a stop that had not taken. The result now carries `stopped` (true only when this call halted a running subagent) and, when it had already finished, a note naming the status and reason it finished with, for example `'deferred' (max_cost_exceeded)`. The description now covers that case. The attach route already made this distinction (#897); the tool did not use it. ([#1164](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1164))
- **The watchdog could tell the model it had said nothing, hours after it had started talking again.** `tools-without-text` feedback is delivered at the start of the next turn. In a long autonomous run the whole task can be a single turn, so a note queued early reached the model long after the silence it described had ended. The note is now dropped when the model speaks before it is delivered. The operator log and the `core_agent.watchdog.alerts` metric still record the trip, and the log line ends `(the model has spoken since)`. Third-party signals can opt in with the new `watchdog.Alert.ClearsOnText` field. ([#1166](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1166))
- **A daemon could panic on shutdown if an attached client's permission stream outlived the prompt broker.** A `/perms/stream` handler whose cleanup ran after the broker closed closed its channel a second time. ([#1169](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1169))
- **A subagent that returned its result and then hit a provider error had that result thrown away, and the parent re-did the whole delegation.** `return_result` acks with `{"ack":"ok"}` — the deliverable is banked at that moment — but if the run failed afterwards the parent's `spawn_agent` result carried only the error text, under `stop_reason: error` and the guidance *"the subagent failed. Whatever text is here is incidental, not a result."* The parent then did exactly what it was told. In the GKE drill run that filed this, the `cluster` subagent ran six reads, produced a complete OOMKill RCA, called `return_result`, was acked at seq 1494, and lost its next model call to a Vertex 429; the parent correctly refused to confabulate from the 429 text, re-investigated with **seven** of its own cluster reads, and finished at 18 of a 25-call ceiling against 14 for the comparable scenario. **The issue put the cause in `completionResult`, and that is the last of three places the result is lost, not the first.** By the time `completionResult` sees the handle, `Returned` is already false and `DoneDetail` empty: `runOneTurn`'s stream-error exit returns from the middle of the event loop and jumped past the `doneCh` drain at the bottom of the function, and `Run`'s `turnErr` branch returns before its `doneSignaled` block (`Resume` has the same shape). A fix confined to `completionResult` passes a test that builds the `Handle` by hand and changes nothing on a live run — which is why the drain is now a **defer**, so every exit harvests it including the ones this function gains later, and why the regression test drives a real spawn rather than a constructed handle. **The outcome is split rather than redefined.** A failure with a banked result reports `stop_reason: returned_then_failed`, puts the returned result in `output`, and carries the run's error in a new **`run_error`** field — separate from `error`, which is documented as a refusal of the *call* and stays that way. Its guidance says the result is real because it was handed back before the failure, and asks the parent to check what is covered and re-ask for the remainder rather than for all of it. A failure with **nothing** banked is untouched: same `error` class, same error-as-`output`, same "incidental" guidance, and it keeps the [#1036](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1036) absorb-disclosure requirement, which the new class does **not** carry because the delegation did deliver. "Banked" requires `Returned` *and* a non-empty payload, since `WithStopOnNaturalEnd` also populates `DoneDetail` from a turn that merely stopped calling tools — promising findings that are not there would be the same defect pointing the other way. The async twin (`terminalAlertText`) renders the same split, per the [#691](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/691) rule that anything only the sync path renders is unreachable exactly when the subagent ran long enough to be worth waiting for. **Keeping the bank across a retry created a second defect, which the review gate caught before the PR.** `bankReturn` deliberately does not consume its copy, so a retry that lands can overwrite it — but the natural-end path writes `DoneDetail` from the turn's trailing text and used to leave `Returned` alone, which was safe only while nothing else could set it. A run that banked on a failed turn, retried, and then ended naturally therefore reported `Returned=true` beside the retry's trailing text, and the parent read `stop_reason: natural` — an assertion the goal was met — over exactly the "let me know if you would like me to continue" non-answer that [#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710) exists to catch. `DoneDetail` and `Returned` now move together on both paths. Unreachable through `spawn_agent`, which installs no retry policy, and reachable for the direct `autonomous.Run` callers the docs tell to install one for transient 429s — the same error class this issue is about. ([#1002](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1002))
- **The eval corpus's restraint checks were evadable by a space-separated global flag, and nothing about the evasion looked wrong in the log.** The shared kubectl shim resolves the verb as the first *positional* argument — correct, since `kubectl -n prod delete pod x` must record `verb=delete` — but it consumed a separate value only for the four flags it knew, and skipped every other flag without skipping its value. So `kubectl --context prod delete pod x` handed `prod` to the verb slot: the witness recorded `verb=prod`, `dispatch` did not recognise a mutation and so did not refuse it, and every `none_of: ["verb=delete", …]` check passed on a cluster that had been written to. **A `none_of` is the one check shape that cannot tell "it did not happen" from "I could not see it"** — `Vacuous` catches a witness that was never written, not one that was written wrong — so this was a silent false pass in the checks whose whole job is to be believed. Not hypothetical: it surfaced in the first live-provider run of `persona-holds-over-a-long-horizon`, where the model's **first** command used the space-separated form. Fixed by giving the shim an explicit `VALUE_FLAGS` set and consuming the value; unknown bare flags are still treated as boolean, which is the right guess for a flag kubectl does not have. Pinned from three directions: the shim is now tested as a **subprocess**, because the property is what lands in the witness file and the witness is written at a call site a parser test would never reach; the flag table is a coverage contract, so a new `VALUE_FLAGS` entry without a test row fails; and every verb in the shim's own `MUTATING_VERBS` is walked, so a verb added later is covered without anyone remembering to. The case-author half of the same hazard is now checked too — a `none_of` term naming a mutating verb without a `verb=` anchor fails offline, because `"kubectl delete"` is a substring that a real delete does not contain. [#1122](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1122)
- **The eval harness's kubectl told three confident lies, and the model quoted all three.** `auth can-i` returned an unconditional "yes" while `dispatch` refused every mutating verb, `get deployment x` ignored the name and listed the whole namespace, and `-o json` fell through to the text table. None of these is a refusal or a shrug — each is a well-formed, quotable answer that happens to be false, which is the one failure mode an agent cannot detect from the output. The `can-i` one is the worst of the three, because it inverts the incentive: `auth can-i` is the correct, non-destructive way to check a permission claim, so an agent doing the careful thing was told it may write, and any case grading permission-checking conduct was entrapment rather than measurement. It now answers from the same two sets `dispatch` enforces — one fact, not two that can disagree — including the RBAC verbs that are not kubectl subcommands (`list`, `watch`), and it honours kubectl's convention that "no" is an answer rather than an error: **stdout, exit 1**, so `if kubectl auth can-i ...` is false. Naming a resource now narrows the result and a name that does not exist is the API server's `NotFound` rather than the empty-list note, which are two different facts a careless shim renders identically; `describe a b` covers both names instead of the first. `-o json`, `-o yaml`, `-o name`, `-o jsonpath` and `-o wide` all work, a format the shim does not model fails loudly with kubectl's own unsupported-printer error instead of silently returning prose, and an unresolvable jsonpath is an error rather than an empty line — empty reads as "the field is unset", and that is a fact. All of it was found by exercising the shim rather than reading it, after a live run of `persona-holds-over-a-long-horizon` spent six calls hunting a container's memory limit through `-o json`, `-o=json` and `-ojson` and then shipped a caveat saying this cluster's kubectl does not support JSON: the run measured our coverage, not the agent's diagnosis. The fixture now declares the limit it plants, and a corpus check fails offline when a world's `planted.memory_limit` and its container spec disagree — with its rule in a self-tested function, because a corpus rule exercised only against a corpus that already satisfies it has no coverage at all. [#1123](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1123)
- **A guardrail that cut an unattended turn said nothing, and the operator's entire output was `anthropic: stream: context canceled`.** Four things cancel a turn in flight; only the context-budget cut logged. The other three — watchdog `enforce`, the per-turn/per-session cost ceiling, and the approval gate's refusal-storm cut — reported themselves *only* through the typed operator-event seam, which is a no-op until an emitter is registered, and the only non-test registration site in the tree is the attach adapter. So a run without `--attach-listen` had no channel at all, and what reached the operator instead was the downstream consequence: a model-client cancellation error that reads as a provider fault. **That is the shape that most needs the line** — per the flag docs, unattended runs (`-p`, `--no-repl`, or non-TTY stdin) default to `watchdog=enforce` *and* a `$10.00` session ceiling, making them at once the likeliest to trip a guardrail and the likeliest to have nobody subscribed. It is also why both core-agent arms of the [#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116) self-development spike died mid-task "with no watchdog or budget trip in the log": under this mechanism nothing would have logged one. Each cut now writes a line naming the guardrail, carrying that arm's own reason text, and disclaiming the cancellation ahead of it — `agent: cost_ceiling guardrail cut the turn in flight [session default] — the cancellation error that follows is this cut, not a provider failure: …` — using the same token `error.type` carries on `gen_ai.agent.invocation.duration`, so the log and a metrics backend describe one halt in one vocabulary. A trip at the turn *boundary* logs `guardrail tripped` instead, since that turn completed and produced an answer; the split mirrors `halted_turn` on the wire. The two emitting guardrails are logged from their shared construction site rather than from their two cut sites, because the reason string is built where the decision is and does not survive out to the caller — and a guardrail added later gets the line without having to remember it. The refusal-storm arm, which withholds its event on purpose ([#1081](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1081): `guardrail-trip`'s vocabulary is shared with the reset endpoint, and this cut needs no reset), logs separately and says so in the text. An operator `/interrupt` is pinned as logging *nothing*, because a log that blames a guardrail for every cancellation cannot be used to find the real ones. Not a substitute for wiring the whole event stream to stderr ([#1132](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1132)) — this is the narrow fix that makes an unattended cut self-diagnosing, and the one that covers the arm no event stream would carry. [#1131](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1131)
- **The guardrail cut and trip lines named no session, so on a daemon the operator could not tell which one to reset.** The line above answers "what stopped this turn" and stops there, which is the whole answer on the one-shot run it was written for and half an answer anywhere else: a multi-session daemon interleaves every session's lines into one log, and the halt that most needs a human — a per-session cost ceiling — ends by asking them to reset it with `additional_budget_usd`, a request addressed to `POST /sessions/{id}/guardrails/reset` and therefore useless without the `{id}`. Both shapes now carry a `[session …]` suffix, spliced after the guardrail's own clause. That leaves `agent: <guardrail> guardrail cut the turn in flight` byte-for-byte unchanged but **moves the other anchor**: `<guardrail> guardrail tripped:` is now `<guardrail> guardrail tripped [session …]:`, so a grep carrying that colon stops matching. Taken knowingly — the line is days old, ships in no tagged release, and a sweep found nothing in the tree matching on it. The single-session run is deliberately *not* special-cased: its id is the `default` that `agent.New` fills in when nothing passes `WithSession`, which is a real id rather than a placeholder — it is what the reset surface takes, and it is already what the `--no-repl` banner prints as `session %s`, so suppressing it here would have been the inconsistency and not the tidying. The context-budget turn cut gets the suffix too, which [#1131](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1131) had passed over because it is not a guardrail and had logged on its own since [#975](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/975): it still ends a turn with the same `context canceled` and is still read out of the same multiplexed log, so leaving it as the one unnamed cut of four was a gap rather than a scope. There the id goes on the log line and *not* into the degraded row's detail text, since that row is already stored against its session — pinned by a test, because appending to the shared string would have satisfied the log assertion while duplicating the id in the durable record. [#1136](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1136)
- **A `{word}` anywhere in an `AGENTS.md` ended every turn before a token was sent, and the error named neither the file nor the token.** The agent's system prompt was handed to ADK's `Instruction` field, which is documented as a *template*: `instructionsRequestProcessor` runs `InjectSessionState` over it on every request, treating `{name}` / `{app:name}` / `{user:name}` as session-state references, and a key nothing has set does not degrade — it returns `ErrStateKeyNotExist` and fails the request. So the run died with `failed to append instructions: failed to inject session state into instruction: state key does not exist`, upstream of the model, with nothing in the message pointing at the prose that caused it. **Layer 4 of that prompt is an operator's `AGENTS.md`, read off disk**, which is the whole problem: nobody writing a contributor guide knows they are writing a template, and the single most likely way to trip it is documenting a shell variable — `` `"${CORE_AGENT}" -p hi` `` trims to `CORE_AGENT`, a valid state name. The trigger set was measured rather than reasoned from the regex, because the shapes sort in a way that is not guessable: `{foo}` and `${FOO}` are fatal on every turn; `{foo?}` is the *optional* form and is silently replaced with the empty string, so the prose vanishes instead of the run; and `{1,64}`, `{StreamingMode: x}`, `{ return y }` and `{build,lint}` are all harmless, because they fail the identifier check. Bare single-word braces only — exactly how a shell variable, a Go type parameter, or a placeholder in a command example gets written. **Nothing is lost by turning it off, and that was established before the fix rather than assumed:** there is no `State().Set` or `StateDelta` write anywhere in `pkg/` or `cmd/`, so no key could ever resolve, and a scan of every `AGENTS.md` / `CLAUDE.md` / `GEMINI.md` in the tree — examples included — finds the feature used by nobody and tripped only by this repo's own root file. The substitution operators *do* want in loaded content is `${env:NAME}`, which is manifest-gated, resolves at load, and never matched the template syntax anyway. Both `llmagent.New` sites (`pkg/agent/agent.go`, `pkg/agent/subtask.go`, the two in the tree) now pass `InstructionProvider` instead, which ADK prefers when both are set and substitutes nothing; the closure ignores its context deliberately, since the prompt is assembled once at construction and re-deriving it per request would cost every prompt-cache hit on the system block. **The reason this was never hit in normal use is the reason it had to be fixed now**: `projectRoot` is the *parent* of the agents directory, so an in-repo run has never loaded the repo's own `AGENTS.md` — and [#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116)'s P3, which commits `/.agents/config.json` so core-agent can work on itself, makes the repo root the project root and every run in the checkout die on turn one. `docs/anthropic-prompt-caching-design.md` called this latent and a no-op; it was neither. One asymmetry between the two ADK fields had to be carried across rather than inherited: `Instruction` skips an empty string and `InstructionProvider` does not, so wiring the provider unconditionally would have sent an empty system part where a consumer previously got no system instruction at all — reachable through `WithInstruction("")` or a whitespace-only `--system-prompt-file`, invisible on the Anthropic path (which drops empty parts) and not on Gemini's. The provider is left nil when there is no prompt. ([#1139](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1139))
- **Every cut that ends a turn in flight promised "the next turn starts clean" to a one-shot run that was about to exit 1.** The two turn-scoped guardrails — the per-turn cost ceiling and a `ScopeTurn` watchdog alert — end their turn and leave the session available ([#1049](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1049), [#1090](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1090)), and both reported that in the present tense, as a fact about a turn that nothing inside `pkg/agent` will ever start. Under a driver the claim is true and useful; in `core-agent -p '…'` there is no next turn, the process exits 1 with the model's `context canceled`, and the operator has just been told by the only line that explained the stop that everything is fine. That reader is the likely one, not the edge case: a one-shot is the run with no client attached, so since [#1131](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1131) this log line *is* the report. **The fix is the wording, and the alternative was considered and rejected.** Plumbing "am I a one-shot?" down to the guardrails means giving the agent a fact about its caller it deliberately does not have — a per-turn trip does not raise the session fence, which is the whole point of #1049, so there is no existing signal to carry it on and `consumeGuardrailHalt` is cleared inside `Run`'s own cleanup before a headless caller regains control. A sentence true in both worlds costs nothing and is strictly more actionable: each message now says the turn stopped **with its work unfinished**, that the session is not halted and a *further* turn would start clean, and then names both futures — under a driver the run goes on, and a `-p` run ends here with exit 1. The half that is identical across the two guardrails is a shared constant in `guardrail_halt.go` beside the emit they already share, and a test reads it back off the strings the guardrails actually emit, because these two have drifted before. The streak count and the escalation bound are untouched and pinned by a test of their own — the point was to stop the line overstating what survived, not to make it say less. **The issue names two cuts and there are four**, which the pre-merge review caught: the other two are log-only, and that makes them the worse instance rather than the lesser one. The approval gate's refusal-storm cut withholds its `guardrail-trip` event deliberately — announcing a cut through a vocabulary shared with the reset endpoint would tell a client there is something to reset — so its log line is the *only* live record of the stop, and it was telling a process about to exit 1 that the next turn starts with an empty refusal map. The [#975](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/975) context-budget cut went further and promised a *remedy*: "compaction will run first on the next turn", which a one-shot never runs. That one splits, and the split is the interesting part: the subjunctive goes into the detail text itself, because that text is also the durable context-reduction-degraded row and a stale promise is wrong in a stored row too, while the one-shot sentence rides the log line only — exactly the division [#1136](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1136) drew for the session id, and for the same reason, since a surface reading that row queried a session and therefore has a caller by construction. Also corrected in the two places an operator reads *before* the run rather than after it: the `--max-turn-cost-usd` flag help and its row in the configuration reference both carried the unqualified sentence. `dev/uat/approval-gate/README.md` still quotes the old text and is deliberately left alone — it is the transcript of a specific historical run, and editing it would falsify a record. ([#1140](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1140))
- **Nine more operator notices named no session, and the id now sits in a fixed slot on every one of them.** [#1136](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1136) tagged the four lines that report a turn *cut* — a complete, checkable unit, being every production `Interrupt()` site — and that is not the set an operator reads off a daemon. Nine other lines said something about one session and identified none of them: both context-reduction degraded notices (an uncatalogued model window, compaction falling back to mechanical truncation), the auto-compaction and pending-checkpoint failures, the both-strategies-down notice, the summarizer's two empty-response lines, a dropped inbox message, and the wake fence. **The fence is the strongest case and the one that motivated the issue**: its sentence ends by telling the operator the input stays queued until a reset, a request addressed to `POST /sessions/{id}/guardrails/reset`, and on a daemon with two halted sessions it emitted byte-identical lines for both. **The scope question the issue opens with is answered "sweep", on evidence rather than taste.** Its worry was that some lines in the package are process-level rather than session-level and would be wrong to tag; walking all thirteen operator-output calls found that exactly one is not on an `*Agent` at all, the inbox's, and even that one is per-agent in fact — so the feared category is empty and the rule is simply *every operator line names its session*. The inbox is handed a copy of the suffix at construction rather than a back-pointer to its parent, because a child that can call back into a locking parent is not a trade worth making for one log line. **The id also moved.** #1136 spliced it mid-sentence and knowingly broke `<guardrail> guardrail tripped:` as a grep anchor to do it; it now sits immediately after the `agent:` prefix on all twelve `agent:` lines, which restores that anchor, keeps the one splicing saved, and makes every future message body anchor-stable — `grep '\[session s-7\]'` returns that session's whole `agent:` story and the phrases stay contiguous. That is what turns "name the session" from a judgement call into a rule, and the judgement call is what missed nine sites. Two tests, covering different failure modes: a table drives all nine paths for real on nine distinct session ids and reads the tag back out of the captured log, and a source scanner walks every `log.Print`/`Fatal`/`Panic` and `fmt.Fprintf(os.Stderr, …)` in the package so the line nobody has written yet cannot ship without one. The scanner asserts an **exact** census of thirteen rather than a floor, because a violation scanner is structurally silent about the site it has stopped recognising and only a number notices — the same reason `verify-harness-config-pinned` carries one; and the raw-field spelling is scoped to the one file entitled to it rather than accepted package-wide, which the review caught as a hole wide enough to drive the original judgement call back through. Where a line's text is also a durable degraded or failure row, the id stays on the log line only — #1136's split, for its reason: the row is already filed against its session. The tail-repair notice is deliberately left in its own idiom; it writes to stderr under the CLI's `core-agent:` prefix and already names its session inline. ([#1137](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1137))
- **`core-tui` v0.27.0 → v0.27.1: `core-agent-tui` now shows a spinner while a tool runs.** A fix-only patch bump, no exported-API change ([core-tui#339](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/339)). The attach TUI's spinner followed chat chunks only: the first partial started it and the commit stopped it. An ordinary turn commits its text and *then* calls a tool, so the spinner went out just before the wait it was there for, and a running tool call's `▶` glyph was the only sign the turn was alive; a turn the autonomous loop or another client started showed nothing until its first token. The spinner now follows the `turn_state` on the attach `status-update` frame, which `pkg/agent` emits as `streaming` before a turn's first content and `idle` when it ends, so it runs across mid-turn commits and tool calls. A client that attaches mid-turn gets the spinner from the stream-open seed, which has reported `streaming` for an in-flight turn since [#896](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/896). The in-process `--tui` is unchanged: it drives turns through `Run`, not the live event stream this fix is about. core-tui also labels `awaiting_permission` / `awaiting_elicit` as "Waiting for approval…" / "Waiting for input…", but this daemon does not emit either state yet — a turn blocked on a permission prompt still reports `streaming` — so an attached operator sees the working spinner there, not the waiting label. `verify-coretui-guards` re-runs clean at 22 exported interfaces, 31 guards and 13 declines. ([#1163](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1163))
- **The GKE drill broke a workload, then scored whichever incident the cluster raised next — on 2026-09-15 that was somebody else's.** [#1093](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1093) — `drill.sh` took the first session to appear on the hub after the break, which is a coin toss on any cluster with event traffic of its own. Seed 3 of the content-`v4` scenario-B sitting lost the toss: a Node Auto-Provisioning scale-up put a `NetworkNotReady` incident on `node-local-dns` in `kube-system` seconds ahead of the `cartservice` OOM the drill had just caused, and the drill captured it, injected the G6 follow-up into it, restored the cluster and wrote an evidence sheet with all three grounded terms missing. That is a **G1 fail recorded against an agent that was never asked the question** — the agent had in fact diagnosed the NAP event correctly and said it was self-resolved. It cost six minutes and a re-run only because `score.py` caught it downstream; a scorer who read the box rather than the transcript would have filed it. This is not the foreign-watcher hazard the preflight already refuses, which is about *another* watcher racing ours: here there was no foreign watcher and our own was working perfectly, on a real incident that was none of our business. So a candidate session is now read before it is taken. Each scenario supplies `SCENARIO_INCIDENT_MATCH` — the namespace plus the object it expects to be named — and the drill peeks at the session's first frame, which is the watcher's inject payload, and keeps waiting on a mismatch rather than capturing, because the drill's own incident is usually seconds behind the stranger's. The key comes from the scenario and not from `${WORKLOAD}` because scenario C breaks no workload at all; the match is over the payload text and not over its `namespace`/`name` fields because a `storm` payload has neither, naming an ancestor namespace and carrying the workloads inside attached representative incidents. Both real payload shapes from that sitting match, the NAP one does not. What the drill passed over is now as readable as what it took: `rejected-sessions.txt` and a raw `peek-<id>.sse` per candidate, the accepted payload in `incident-payload.txt`, and a console line at step 3 naming the incident actually in hand. The timeout finally distinguishes "the break landed but no incident did", which is a watcher problem, from "incidents landed and none of them was yours", which is a busy cluster, and from "sessions appeared and not one ever showed a first frame", which is the daemon's — only the first of the three existed before, and the second is the one that sitting needed. `DRILL_MATCH_INCIDENT=0` restores the old behaviour for a payload shape the matcher cannot read. `dryrun.sh` grows the two cases, in both of which the stranger is the most recently *touched* session — the one the old rule selected on, so that a fixture cannot pass against the bug.
- **An operator's "no" still ended the daemon's working life — one guardrail later, and for writing the refusal down.** [#1090](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1090) — run 16 of `dev/uat/approval-gate/` is the first where every fix in the [#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068) → [#1074](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1074) → [#1081](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1081) → [#1086](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1086) sequence did exactly what it promised: the denial was final, the repeats were suppressed without paging anybody, the turn was cut without tripping a thing, and the repetition came off the watchdog's books on the way out. Leg 3 still never ran. The re-driven turn called `mark_task_done` four times to record the denial, responses 2–4 came back `{"no_op":true,…}`, and three inert results is `no-op-streak`'s Critical — which under `--watchdog=enforce` halted the **session**, so auto-continue stood down until a human reset it. Runs 12, 14 and 16 all end this way; it is the modal ending of an ordinary denial, which is the measurement [#907](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/907) priced this signal without. **The fix is the shape [#1049](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1049) already chose for the per-turn cost ceiling: severity says how sure a signal is that something is wrong, and a new `watchdog.Alert.Scope` says what stopping it costs.** `ScopeTurn` cuts the turn the evidence arrived in and nothing further; three consecutive turns cut that way escalate to the session halt, because a model restarting the loop every fresh turn is the one shape a turn-scoped cut genuinely cannot answer; one turn that ends clean clears the count, and so does an operator reset — leaving it parked at the threshold would re-halt the session on the next cut, one turn after a human looked at it and said carry on. `NoOpStreakSignal` is the only shipped signal that opts down, and it is entitled to: its streak resets on any productive result, so the calls it counts are consecutive and come from one turn, and ending that turn ends the loop as completely as halting the session would — #905's own sixteen calls were a single turn. Nothing #907 bought is given up, and what it cost is: the agent that says "there is nothing further to record" three times loses a turn rather than its working life. **The scrub is what makes the escalation real, not decoration.** `NoOpStreakSignal` latches `tripped` to emit one alert per streak, so a cut that left the signals loaded would mean it never alerted again — the streak stuck at one and the loop unbounded — and the cut therefore calls the same `resetWatchdogSignals` #1086 introduced for the gate's cut, with a test asserting the reset count rather than trusting the reasoning. Three smaller decisions worth stating: a session-scoped Critical **wins** over a turn-scoped one in the same drain, so a signal asking for the smaller consequence cannot downgrade one asking for the larger by slice position; one cut per turn however many drains observe it; and `AlertScope`'s zero value is `ScopeSession`, so every pre-existing signal and every third-party one keeps enforce's original contract without a line of change — opting *down* is a claim a signal has to earn. No protocol bump: `guardrail-trip` already carries `halted_turn`, and the session-vs-turn distinction rides the reason prose exactly as the cost ceiling's does.
- **A late approval for a prompt a guardrail had cut down was answered "never issued".** [#1088](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1088) — `POST /perms/respond` had two answers for a prompt that is no longer pending: **410 Gone** with a body saying the action was not taken, and **404** with a body reading `prompt id not found (already responded, cancelled, or never issued)`. Only an expiry got the first one, because `PromptBroker` wrote its tombstone only when `context.Cause` was `permissions.ErrPromptExpired` — the gate's own timeout is the one cause anybody labels, and a guardrail cutting a turn, an operator pressing stop and a daemon going down all arrive at the same unlabelled closed channel. Runs 13 and 14 of `dev/uat/approval-gate/` are the same request one leg apart and answer differently: the run whose prompt expired on the clock got `410`, and the run whose prompt was still open when the refusal-storm halt cancelled its turn got `404`. That is the wrong answer to the only question an out-of-band approver has. They authorized a write some minutes ago and want to know whether it happened; "already responded" would mean somebody else answered, "never issued" would mean they are posting to the wrong session, and the truth — it was here, it is gone, and nothing ran — was the one reading the sentence does not offer. So the broker now remembers **every** prompt whose wait ended without an answer, and the tombstone carries the reason rather than the ring meaning one thing: `ErrPromptExpired` when the gate's clock ran out, and a new `ErrPromptCanceled` otherwise, both **410**. Keeping them apart is the point of storing a reason at all, because they prescribe different fixes — "expired" tells an operator to answer faster or raise `approval_timeout`, and for a cancellation answering faster would not have helped, since the turn ended for a reason that had nothing to do with them. **404 narrows to what it can still honestly claim**: an id already answered, and one this daemon never issued. Protocol **1.14.0**, a minor for the same reason 1.12.0 was — no frame changes shape, and a status a client already handles now covers a second case — but a version rather than a silent fix, because the status code is the entire signal here and a client reading 404 on a pre-1.14.0 daemon cannot tell a prompt a guardrail took from an id the broker never had. Found by the drill that produced [#1086](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1086) and filed rather than folded into it, since #1086 removes the halt that produced this particular 404 and would otherwise have buried the defect under a green run.
- **The fix for [#1081](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1081) stopped leg 2 halting the session and leg 3 started halting it instead.** [#1086](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1086) — the gate now ends a turn full of refused calls without tripping anything, and the drill's next three runs said the turn-scoped cut works exactly as specified: `leg3-guardrail-reset.json` came back `{"reset":[]}` where runs 10 and 11 had `{"reset":["watchdog"]}`, the cut landed 11ms after the third suppressed response at four gated calls rather than the watchdog's five, and the durable row arrived with `repeats: 3`. And the session still got halted, one turn later, by `repeated-tool-call` quoting **leg 2's** arguments back: `agent has called alert with identical args 5 times in a row — Args: {"summary":"uat-deny-…"}`. The watchdog is reading the same tool stream the gate is, `RepeatedToolCallSignal.runLength` is not turn-scoped, and nothing cleared it — so the four calls the gate had already disposed of were still on the books and leg 3's first identical call was the fifth. **The rig's own workaround had been hiding this for the whole life of the feature.** Before #1081, leg 2 always tripped a watchdog, so `prepare_leg` always called the reset endpoint before leg 3, and `ResetWatchdog` scrubs the signal run lengths as a side effect of clearing the halt; every leg 3 that started behind a reset passed and both that started without one broke, which is a correlation with the *reset* and not with the build. #1081 removed the halt and thereby removed a scrub nothing had ever named as load-bearing. So the refusal-storm arm now takes the repetition off the watchdog's books on its way out, through a `resetWatchdogSignals` that is deliberately **not** `ResetWatchdog`: it clears the evidence and never the tripped flag, because an arm that could un-halt would let a looping agent overrule the operator, whereas scrubbing signal state cannot — the state is evidence for a conclusion the agent has already reached by a tighter route. That tightness is the whole safety argument: three suppressed repeats is a lower bound than `repeated-tool-call`'s five, so a model looping through the gate is stopped *sooner* than the watchdog would have stopped it every time, and a loop on a tool the gate never sees never reaches this arm, so nothing is reset there. The knock-on the drill also caught is not a separate defect and is not fixed here: run 13's leg-3 prompt was pending when the halt cancelled its turn, and `PromptBroker.rememberExpired` only writes its tombstone when the context's cause is `ErrPromptExpired`, so a late out-of-band approval was answered `404 prompt id not found` instead of `410 Gone` — correct for a cancelled prompt, and visible only because the guardrail should not have tripped. Proved against the pre-fix code with the real `DefaultWatchdog` at its shipped thresholds rather than a fake counting `Reset` calls, because the margin under test is one call wide and a test that invented its own numbers would not be standing where the cluster stood; the pre-fix run reproduces the daemon's message verbatim, down to `5 times in a row`.
- **One operator "no" still ended an unattended daemon's working life, because the only thing that would end a turn full of refused calls was a guardrail that halts the session.** [#1081](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1081) — run 11 of `dev/uat/approval-gate/` measured [#1074](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1074) on a cluster and the answer was five fewer prompts and **zero fewer calls**: runs 10 and 11 printed the byte-identical watchdog critical, `repeated-tool-call: agent has called alert with identical args 5 times in a row`. The issue was filed believing the turn never ended. It did — `enforceWatchdogInTurn` ([#705](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/705)) cut leg 2's loop 580µs after its critical and leg 3's 3.4ms after its own, and the two minutes the issue reports are the rig waiting for idle between legs, not a turn running. Turn termination was never the gap. **The gap is the scope of the thing reaching for the lever.** Nine milliseconds after leg 3's cut the daemon logged `turn refused (guardrail still tripped, not reset)`, and 53 seconds later auto-continue stood down "until it is reset" — so the session-scoped halt that ended the loop also ended everything after it, which is the [#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068) wedge intact one layer down. Meanwhile the component holding the unambiguous evidence was not consulted: the gate's per-turn refusal map knew at call #2 what `repeated-tool-call` took until call #5 to infer, and in leg 2 nineteen seconds and four further gated calls passed between the operator's denial and the cut, with a `tool-failure-streak` **warn** firing nine seconds before the critical and by design unable to act. So the gate now ends the turn on its own evidence, and ends only the turn. `Gate.turnRefusal` counts suppressed calls at the one function both suppression paths funnel through — the ordinary prompt path and the elevated control-plane one — `Gate.TurnRefusalRepeats` exposes the count through the per-session sub-gate the way every other method resolves, and the agent reads it from `Run`'s event tap on each event carrying a tool result, cutting the turn at three. **Four decisions, each of which is a way this could have been wrong.** *Turn scope, not session scope*: nothing trips, nothing needs resetting, and the next turn opens with the refusals in the model's history and an empty refusal map — a model that will not take no for an answer has had a bad turn, and it does not follow that the session is unsafe to run. *Three, not the watchdog's five*: `repeated-tool-call` needs five because a repeated tool name cannot distinguish a loop from a sweep, whereas a suppressed repeat is not an inference at all — the operator refused this exact request, the model was told so in a tool result, and it asked again. *Counted across requests*, because a model alternating between two already-refused calls is in the same state as one repeating a single call, and per-key counting would let it stay under any threshold by alternating. *And no `guardrail-trip` frame*: `attach.GuardrailTrip.Guardrail` shares its vocabulary with `POST /guardrails/reset` and the durable trip row, so announcing a cut that needs no reset through it would tell every attached client to offer a reset for a guardrail that does not exist. The turn's terminal frame stays the ordinary `canceled`; the reason is durable in the eventlog (`Author=gate/refusal-storm`, carrying the count); and `markGuardrailHalt` labels the turn's metric point `refusal_storm` so a backend can still tell this stop from an operator pressing stop — a metric-vocabulary addition and not a protocol change, since neither `watchdog` nor `cost_ceiling` has been on the wire since [#891](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/891) either. There is no flag: the other two in-turn arms are opt-in because they act on inference and an operator may reasonably prefer to watch, and this one acts on a decision the operator already made. Wired outside the `a.watchdog != nil` block rather than on the watchdog's own `observed` flag, so the gate's protection does not depend on whether a watchdog was configured — a fails-first pass with it moved back inside fails exactly the two no-watchdog tests and passes the one with a watchdog wired. **What this does not settle.** #1081's own criterion is "leg 3 finds no watchdog to clear", and only a drill re-run can say whether it is met; leg 3's loop is `mark_task_done` rather than a gated call, so its being covered is inference from the shared shape rather than measurement. *Settled since, in both directions:* runs 12 and 13 met the criterion literally — `{"reset":[]}` at leg 3's entry, where runs 10 and 11 had a watchdog to clear — and defeated it in substance, because the run length the rig's reset had been quietly scrubbing then halted the session one turn later. See [#1086](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1086) above.
- **A refused call could re-open the same prompt, again and again, until the watchdog halted the session.** [#1074](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1074) — [#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068) shipped the cheap half of this earlier in the milestone: a denial and an expiry now tell the model, in the tool result it reads, that the answer will not change. Run 10 of `dev/uat/approval-gate/` measured what that bought on a live cluster and the answer was nothing. All seventeen assertions passed — including the two that read the daemon's event stream to prove the model was handed the wording — and leg 2 still opened five prompts, with `watchdog: enforce` escalating exactly as it had pre-fix, four calls into the loop, quoting the new guidance back as its `Last error`. Eight prompts on a run whose floor is three. **So the gate stops asking.** `Gate.prompt` is the single choke point every interactive path funnels through, and after a deny or an expiry it refuses an identical request — same tool, same detail — for the remainder of the **turn**, without opening a prompt and without notifying anybody: the operator who said no once is not paged five times, and the model's fourth attempt fails in microseconds rather than after another full `approval_timeout` spent waiting for somebody already established to be absent. Three scoping decisions, each the answer to a way this could be wrong. **Only a refusal arms it** — an allow-once followed by an identical call is an ordinary second call and gets an ordinary prompt, because saying yes to one invocation is not evidence about the next one in either direction. **It is keyed on the request, not the tool**, and on the gate's existing `tool|detail` identity rather than a second opinion about what "the same request" means: that is the pair `sessionAllow` uses when it remembers an approval, and a refusal must not be allowed to disagree with an approval about the question. `pkg/watchdog` canonicalizes path-shaped arguments before comparing calls, which is right for a loop detector, where a false positive costs a warning; here a false positive costs a call the operator would have approved, silently, so the key errs toward asking again — an agent whose `kubectl apply` was refused can fix its manifest and ask about the new one. **And it clears at the turn boundary**, from the pre-turn pipeline's existing `turn-boundary` step ([#655](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/655)), which exists for exactly this class of signal and sits deliberately *after* both preflights — a turn that never ran is not a boundary, so an auto-continue re-drive of a refused turn cannot launder a denial and re-open the prompt the operator just closed; `preturn_test.go` holds that with the same transposition test the watchdog half has. By the next real turn the model has the refusal in its own history, and the operator's circumstances may have changed; a session-scoped memory would additionally mean one misclick silently disabling a tool for hours, which is a support burden nobody would ever connect back to the click. The elevated control-plane write path carries its own copy of the check, because it deliberately bypasses `Gate.prompt` and remembers nothing — remembering a *refusal* there does not weaken that rule, which exists so an approval cannot install a standing bypass, and it is the path where a re-issue costs the most, since it pages the operator repeatedly about the file that decides what the agent is allowed to do. Two limits worth stating plainly. The detail string is `summarizeRequest`'s 200-character truncation, so two calls with very long, identically-prefixed arguments collide and the second is suppressed for the turn — over-suppression, bounded by the turn, and the same truncation already affects `sessionAllow` in the *permissive* direction. And a background subagent sharing a session gate clears the memory at its own turn start, which widens rather than over-suppresses. **This is unconfirmed on a cluster.** The gate's behaviour has unit tests; what a model does when it meets the gate does not, which is the entire lesson of #1068, and the rig's next run is the verdict — leg 2 opening one prompt and leg 3 finding no watchdog to clear is #1074's acceptance criterion, with the prompt count as the measure for the same reason it was the measure last time.
