- **A retry test that waited for the RPC to be sent, rather than for its failure to be written down, could lose an attempt and then blame the clock.** [#1077](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1077) — `internal/vertexcache`'s fake increments its call counter inside `Caches.Create`, but `doInit` records the failure, arms the backoff deadline, and reopens the init gate only after that call returns. Three tests resumed on the counter and immediately drove the next `Init`, which could land in the gap, hit the already-initializing guard, fire no RPC, and schedule no retry — leaving nobody to move the counter and a `waitFor` to spend its full ten seconds on a lost attempt that reads, in CI, as a slow one. It cost two red `test` jobs on an unrelated pull request. The waits now key on the manager's own failure count, which is incremented in the same critical section as the rest of the bookkeeping, and one of the three sites was not flaky at all but falsely green: it credited the backoff for a silence the init guard was producing. The window itself was already known and documented on the transient path ([#499](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/499)); this is that lesson applied to the retry path.
- **The cursor a live-tail pump reads from was parked on a field the next reconnect could overwrite under it.** [#1075](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1075) — `pkg/attach`'s broadcaster starts one pump goroutine per session, lazily, for the first subscriber; when the last subscriber leaves, the pump is cancelled and the *next* subscriber starts a successor. The sequence each pump begins its `Watch` from lived on the broadcaster struct, written under the mutex by the `register` that spawned the pump and read without the mutex by the pump goroutine itself — and between the `go` statement and that read, a whole detach-and-reconnect cycle can land and write the field for somebody else's pump. That is a data race in the plain sense, and the race detector found it the way races are usually found: as an unrelated CI failure, on a pull request touching a different package, attributed to whichever test happened to be running. The cursor is immutable for the life of a pump, so it is now handed to the goroutine as an argument — the same shape the existing generation stamp already uses — and the shared field is gone rather than locked, because there is no second reader left to synchronize with.
- **A prompt too small to cache was retried for seven and three-quarter minutes, as though waiting could make it bigger.** `internal/vertexcache` retries a failed `Caches.Create` on a bounded backoff, and [#707](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/707) is why: a daemon that comes up before its Workload-Identity binding has propagated gets a 403, the config was right the whole time, and the pre-#707 sticky failure made one live GKE session pay full input price on all 365K of its billed input tokens. That reasoning covers every failure time can fix, and exactly one it cannot. Explicit caching has a per-model floor — 4096 tokens on `gemini-3.7-flash` — and what gets cached is the agent's system instruction plus its tool declarations, which is a property of the configuration and does not grow while the process runs, so attempt 6 carries precisely the tokens attempt 1 did. The manager now classifies that answer as permanent on sight and logs one line instead of six. **The cost this fixes is diagnostic, not monetary.** Six doomed RPCs are cheap; six lines reading `Caches.Create failed (attempt 1 of 6; retrying no sooner than 15s from now)` are not, because `doInit` runs on a goroutine fired when a turn starts its first model request, so each one is printed *next to a turn start* and reads like the cause of a slow turn it has nothing to do with. During the [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647) approval-gate UAT a leg whose prompt opened 185 seconds late had one of these two seconds before it, the correlation was clean across all three legs, and ruling it out cost an hour of reading `manager.go`. The new line is also written to be recognisable as the **retraction** of the startup line's `context cache: enabled` claim — same words, opposite direction — and it says there is nothing to fix, because there is not: the agent runs uncached and every turn still works. Two things about the predicate are deliberate. It keys on the provider's stated reason (`INVALID_ARGUMENT` paired with "minimum token count") and **never on the number**, since the minimum is per-model and Google has moved it before, so a predicate that knows 4096 is one that silently stops working the day it changes. And it is narrow enough that a 403 keeps its full schedule — there is a test asserting exactly that, because over-matching here is how #707 comes back. This is the small half of what the approval-gate rig found; the serious half was [#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068). ([#1067](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1067))
- **One "no" from a human, and nobody else involved, was enough to wedge a gated daemon.** Observed on the cluster during the [#647](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/647) gated UAT: the rig denied one out-of-band approval, the model re-issued the identical `alert` call, the retry went unanswered and expired, three consecutive tool failures tripped a `tool-failure-streak` warning, five identical calls tripped `repeated-tool-call` critical, three inert `mark_task_done`s tripped `no-op-streak`, and `watchdog: enforce` halted the session — after which every turn was refused until something POSTed `guardrails/reset`. **Every individual piece of that was working.** The gate refused the call, the timeout fired, the watchdog caught a genuine spin and stopped it. What was wrong is what the model was told: `alert denied by user: pager` reports an event and says nothing about what follows from it, and to a model carrying an instruction an event is an obstacle to route around, the obvious route being the same call again. The expiry is the worse half — nothing in "approval request expired with no answer" suggests that a *second* attempt will also go unanswered, which on an unattended daemon is exactly what happens, with a fresh page to the operator each time. Both refusals now say the thing the model cannot infer and the runtime knows for certain: a denial is **final for the call it answered** ("do not re-issue it; choose a different approach, or end the turn and report the refusal"), and an expiry means **nobody is attached to answer a retry either** ("do not re-issue this call; end the turn and report that the action is still pending approval"). The expiry message keeps its operator half — the timeout, the tool, the detail, and the `/perms/stream` way out — because the two audiences read the same string out of the same log, and losing the remediation to make room for the instruction would trade one silent failure for another. This is guidance and not a gate, deliberately: the issue's second layer — the gate refusing an identical request outright for the rest of the turn after a deny or an expiry, so an operator who declined once is not paged three times — is a real behaviour change with a real edge case (a legitimate retry after conditions change) and wants its own argument, so it is **not** here. The same run turned up a smaller thing worth fixing on the way past: the watchdog quoted `permissions: permissions: approval request expired…`, because `ErrPromptExpired` names the package itself — it is matched as a sentinel and travels as a `context.Cause`, where no wrap of ours reaches it — and the gate wrapped it a second time on the way out. Verification is on the cluster rather than only in the package: `dev/uat/approval-gate/`'s deny and expire legs now each read the daemon's own event stream and assert the agent received the sentence, on the argument that #1068 was a defect in what a model was told and a fix nobody checks against a live gate is a fix that regresses quietly. The rig still prints how many prompts the gate opened across the run, which is the honest measure of whether the model took the advice — a sentence is not a gate, and three is one per leg. ([#1068](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1068))
- **A daemon that had been up for six and a half hours announced its self-heal as a boot scan.** The best single piece of evidence the 2026-09-14 A1 soak produced was an unattended recovery: a provider `INVALID_ARGUMENT` killed a turn mid-flight at 19:18:52 with nobody attached, the in-lifetime retry driver ([#575](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/575) defect B) noticed the stranded tail 4m17s later, queued a continuation, and the session went on to add two more turns — no restart, no operator. The line announcing it read `core-agent: auto-continue boot scan: queued continuations for 1 session(s)`, and the pod had **zero restarts and 6h38m of uptime** at that moment. `AutoContinueBootScan` and `AutoContinueStartupSession` are each fired by two drivers — once at boot, then on the `retry_interval` timer — and every message either one writes was prefixed `boot scan:` / `startup:` regardless of which driver fired it, because the prose was written when there was only one. The audience for a line printed at 19:23 on an unattended daemon is someone reconstructing a night they did not watch; told the process rebooted, they are holding two facts that cannot both be true, and they go looking for a crash that never happened. It cost a detour through `main.go` to establish that the daemon had been up the whole time while writing the run note, which is the same class of defect box A2 of [#1042](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1042) names: a recovery legible only to someone who reads the source is not an observable one. Each pass now takes the trigger that fired it and attributes its own output — `auto-continue retry:` and `auto-continue startup retry:` on the timer path, the existing wording unchanged on the boot path, and the breaker standing down "this pass" rather than "this boot" when it trips on a tick. Deliberately scoped to what is **read**, not to what is **counted**: the `agent_boot_log` row, the crash-loop breaker, the per-session single-retry window and the cumulative cap all keep their per-boot semantics and their existing call sites, because the guards were never confused about which driver they were bounding — only the reader was. The two new exported entry points (`AutoContinueRetryScan`, `AutoContinueStartupRetry`) are the same pass under the same guards; `AutoContinueRetryLoop`'s callers in `cmd/core-agent/main.go` are what changed. ([#1066](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1066))
- **`--token` never took a token, and the failure mode for reading it as if it did was a bare 401.** The flag takes the **NAME of an environment variable** holding the bearer token, and that indirection is the entire security property it exists for: a secret passed on a command line is visible in `ps ax` to every user on the box, lands in shell history, and is copied into whatever process listing a crash reporter or container runtime happens to capture. The name said the opposite. Observed 2026-07-13 on the v2.6 GKE demo drive as `--token "${SRE_TOKEN}"` — the shape a careful person types, quoted and expanded by the shell — which the binary then looked up as a variable name, found nothing, and sent an empty `Authorization` header, producing a 401 with nothing in it connecting the refusal to the flag. The flag is now **`--token-env`** at all three call sites, which is two more than [#947](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/947) names: the same defect was in `core-agent attach` and `core-agent ls`, which is what happens when a flag is declared three times instead of resolved once. `--token` **stays as a deprecated alias with unchanged semantics** rather than being removed, because a rename that breaks every existing invocation and every deployment manifest quoting one is a worse bug than the one being fixed; it warns and names the replacement. The resolution now lives in one place (`internal/attachclient.ResolveTokenEnv`) and diagnoses **both** ways to get this wrong before the request goes out. A value that is not a valid env-var name draws "takes the NAME of an env var, not the token itself" — and deliberately **does not echo the value**, because it is almost certainly the secret and printing it would put it in exactly the scrollback and log aggregator the indirection exists to keep it out of, turning a 401 into a leak. A well-formed name that resolves to nothing draws its own warning naming the 401 it is about to cause, which is the likelier failure in a container where the variable was simply never plumbed into the pod spec. Both are warnings rather than hard errors, because running with no token at all is a supported posture — Unix-socket attach, and a daemon started without `--attach-token` ("Posture B") — so an empty resolution cannot be made fatal without breaking it, and the no-token path stays silent so those runs do not grow a spurious warning. One limit is pinned in the tests rather than left to be rediscovered: a GitHub PAT is `[A-Za-z0-9_]` throughout, so no syntactic check can tell a name-shaped secret from a name, and the unset-variable warning is what catches that case. ([#947](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/947))
- **Every context check in the tree sat on a turn boundary, and the thing that overflows a context window does not wait for one.** Inside a single agentic turn the sequence is: a model call completes and commits its usage, a tool runs and returns a payload, the next model call is built with that payload in it — and nothing evaluated context between the second and third steps ([#975](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/975)). So one large tool result, a full pod log or a wide `kubectl get -o json` or an MCP response over a big cluster, went into the next request while the utilization figure that compaction and `/context` both read still described the comfortable state before it arrived. On the GKE path those results are the normal case rather than an edge case, which is why this is a v3.0 defect and not a theoretical one: the failure it produces is a provider rejection for an oversized request, an error that classifies badly and points nowhere near compaction, arriving after the operator has paid for the turn that built it. `usage.Tracker` now carries the unmeasured tail: `AddPendingContextBytes` records the size of anything appended since the last measurement, `ContextWindowUsed` returns the measured input count **plus** an estimate of it, and `AppendUsage` clears the tail because the next call's `InputTokens` is a real measurement of the whole conversation as sent. Five decisions are load-bearing and each has a test that fails without it. **The ratio is three bytes per token, not the usual four**, and the direction is the entire point: what is being estimated is JSON, YAML and log output, which tokenize worse than the prose that rule of thumb comes from, and the errors are not symmetric — under-counting means the threshold does not fire and the defect survives, while over-counting means one compaction runs early, which is visible and priced. **Only function responses are measured.** Model text is already counted by the usage record for the call that produced it, and the provider's own number beats anything derived locally; tool results are the gap precisely because they enter history between two measurements with nothing reporting their size. **Bytes appended before any turn has landed are not reported as usage**, because with no measured floor to add them to the estimate alone is a number with no relationship to the window, and 0 keeps the established "unknown, suppress the segment" contract. **The new `ContextWindowUsedEstimated()` says which kind of number it returned** — the assessment's note on this is right that a utilization figure which can only be stale is worse than one admitting it is a guess — and is exported on both `usage.Tracker` and `Agent`; the bundled TUI does not draw the distinction yet, deliberately, because [#891](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/891)'s lesson is that shipping one side of a client-visible change regresses the other, so the substrate reports it now and the client reads it when it lands. And **the assumption about window size stays where [#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974) put it**: this path calls `compactionWindowSize()`, so an uncatalogued model gets the assumed 128K here too rather than silently skipping the check. Two arms act on the estimate, both inside the turn. Compaction is marked pending the moment the compactor's own threshold is crossed, instead of at the end of a turn that may still have a dozen tool calls to run — the threshold is unchanged, only when it is read. Past `ContextBudgetHardCeiling` (0.95) the turn is cut, with compaction already pending, so the next turn reduces context and carries on; 0.95 sits above every shipping threshold (0.85 frontier, 0.65 mid, 0.35 small) so the ordinary path always acts first and this is a backstop for the one case the first arm cannot cover, a single result crossing both lines at once. The 5% margin is deliberate — the number being compared is an estimate of exactly the bytes nobody measured, and the request also carries a system instruction and tool declarations this does not count. The cut is announced as a `context-reduction-degraded` row with the new `operation: turn-cut`, carrying the estimate, the window and the unmeasured byte count, which means **no attach protocol change at all**. It is deliberately not a `guardrail-trip`: `cost_ceiling` and `watchdog` latch a session and wait for an operator to reset it, while this heals itself on the next turn, so borrowing that vocabulary would render a "go reset your session" affordance for a condition with nothing to reset — the terminal frame is the ordinary `canceled`, as for any interrupted turn. It latches **per turn** rather than per process, inverting [#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974)'s rule for the right reason: `window-unknown` and `mechanical-compaction` describe states, and a state re-announced every turn is one operators filter out, but a cut is an *attempt*, and a session cutting one every turn is a very different report from a session that cut one once. A tool-layer truncation wrapper was considered and rejected — it is the [#223](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/223) Phase 4 trap, where a process-level tool wrapper callback cannot see per-session trackers — as was mid-turn compaction, which risks splitting a function-call/function-response pair across the boundary. ([#975](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/975))
- **Compaction had two dependencies a long unattended run can lose, and it lost both silently: an uncatalogued model disabled it outright, and a 429 storm broke the only escape hatch from context growth.** Neither defect is visible while anything is going well, which is why both survived into a milestone about proving autonomy ([#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974)). The first: `DefaultCompactor.ShouldCompact` divides used tokens by the model's context window, and for a model the pricing catalogue has never heard of — a Vertex publication name, a proxy id, a model newer than the build — `ContextWindowSize()` returns 0, which the ratio read as *not full yet*. Compaction therefore never fired at all: absent, silent, and observable only when the provider eventually hard-failed on an oversized request several thousand turns later, at which point nothing in the daemon log points at compaction. That is the worst shape a safety mechanism can have. The window is now assumed to be **128,000 tokens** (`agent.AssumedContextWindowSize`) and the substitution is announced once, naming the model id, because the operator's fix — pin a catalogued id, or accept the guess — cannot be chosen from *“compaction is degraded”* alone. The number is deliberately wrong in the safe direction: guessing too large lands the threshold past a wall the provider enforces and kills the session, which is precisely the [#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974) symptom, while guessing too small only buys extra compactions — real work and real money, but the run survives and every one of them is visible and priced. It is **not** pushed down into `usage.ContextWindowSizeFor`, where 0 still means unknown and `/context` still renders it as unknown; a tracker reporting a fabricated window would make every consumer display a made-up percentage as though it were measured. The assumption belongs to the component that has to act without the answer. The second defect: compaction summarizes by *calling the model*, so the same shed that is breaking turns breaks compaction, the failure backoff doubles out to 32 turns, and history grows the entire time — the only strategy depended on the resource that was already failing. After **two** consecutive summarizer failures (two, not one: a single failure is frequently a transient the next attempt clears, and spending a whole compaction window on a truncation a retry would have summarized properly is a real loss), compaction falls back to **mechanical truncation** — no model call, a deterministic digest of the dropped window: a ledger of the tool calls that ran with their arguments but not their results, most recent first and capped, plus the conversation tail verbatim inside a fixed character budget. The digest opens by telling the model in plain terms that it is reading a truncation, that detail *has* been lost, and that it should re-run a tool rather than guess at what a dropped result said — the [#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710) lesson that prose next to the data is what changes a model's behaviour. It writes an ordinary boundary event (`CustomMetadata["compaction"] = "summary"`), so the read side is untouched; a second key `compaction_mechanical` marks it for a post-mortem that needs to know which kind of boundary it is looking at, because the two are not equally trustworthy. Success clears the cooldown but **keeps** the failure count, which leaves exactly one summarizer attempt per context-refill cycle — that attempt is simultaneously the cost and the recovery probe. If the mechanical path fails too, that is a `context-reduction-failed` row: both strategies are down. Both conditions announce themselves on a **new** `context-reduction-degraded` event rather than a new `operation` value on the existing failure row, because *“did not run”* and *“ran, but worse”* read oppositely during an incident. The author is shared on purpose so a client already matching on it sees both; the event name is what separates them, and that forced a real tightening — `attach.ContextReductionFailure` matched on the author alone, so a degraded row would have been read as a failure until an `InvocationID` check was added. Each row fires at most once per process: both describe a state, not an attempt, and a state re-announced every turn is one operators filter out. Three honest limits. 128,000 is a judgement call, not a measurement, and the day a provider ships a smaller-windowed model that is not in the catalogue it will be wrong in the unsafe direction. Mechanical truncation is lossy in a way a summary is not — it is what keeps an unattended run alive until someone looks at it, not a steady state, and a degraded row on a session anyone cares about is a reason to intervene. And `TestDefaultCompactor_ShouldCompact_UnknownWindowSkips` was **deleted**, not fixed: it asserted the defect, having pinned the 0-means-skip behaviour as though it were the intent. ([#974](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/974))
- **A delegation that died took the disclosure with it: the parent quietly did the work itself and the answer scored full marks.** Four runs in the 38-run GKE drill archive lost their `cluster` subagent to a provider error, and the failure mode is not the one the shape suggests — it is not a dead run, it is a run that quietly stops being a delegated run. `dev/trajectory` makes it a column rather than a footnote: the 2026-09-11 run that scored 6/6 with a dead subagent is **6 steps with 1 in the child**, against 11–12 steps with 9–10 in the child for every other run of the same scenario ([#1028](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1028)). The parent absorbed the entire investigation, answered well, and the operator reading that answer had no way to know a delegation had been planned and had not happened — which is the thing that matters, because the whole claim of a specialist subagent is that its answer came from the specialist. One of the four disclosed unprompted; the measure that separates the two (`delegation-disclosed` / `delegation-undisclosed`) exists precisely because neither behaviour was required of the parent. Now it is, in the only place that has ever changed a model's behaviour here: a sentence in prose, at the tool-result boundary, next to the payload ([#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710), [#1031](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1031)). `background.AbsorbDisclosure` — *“If you do this work yourself instead, say so in your final answer: name the subagent and say what went wrong with it. An operator reading only your answer has no other way to know the delegation did not happen.”* — is appended to the `guidance` line on the two stop reasons that leave the parent holding nothing it can use (`error`, `no_return`), to a spawn refused before it ever launched, and to the terminal `[Background reports]` alert on those same reasons. The async path needs it more than the sync one, not less: an alert carries no `guidance` field at all, so a fire-and-continue delegation that died would otherwise reach the parent as an error string and an enum, which is the exact shape [#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710) established a model does not act on. It is on those outcomes **only**, and the exclusions are the design: `max_steps` and `budget` handed back real delegated work and a partial is not an absorbed delegation, `deferred` is not over so absorbing is the wrong move, `stopped` is something the parent asked for, and a caveat on every outcome is a caveat on none. Two honest limits. The sentence does not tell the parent to *refuse* the work — whether a parent should retry, downgrade, or say it cannot answer is [#1036](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1036)'s open item 1, and it wants a second occurrence before anyone designs it. And the unit tests here pin wording and routing, not behaviour: this is prose aimed at a language model, so the only real verification is a cluster batch, and delegated failures are rare enough (4 of 38 runs before the [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) retry, 1 of 21 after) that no batch anyone will run produces a disclosure rate worth quoting. Read the next occurrence by eye. ([#1036](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1036))
- **A retry budget of one-per-window handed its single rescue to whichever caller was rejected first, and most retries never said what became of them.** Both defects come out of the same ninety-minute GKE batch that verified [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) (`dev/uat/gke-drill/runs/2026-09-13-std-simian-test-bc-20run-post935.md`), and the first is the one that still killed a delegation: the parent's 429 at 10:14:32 consumed the process-wide cooldown, the subagent's 429 six seconds later was refused a retry, and its `spawn_agent` failure is stamped 745 microseconds after the `NOT retried` line — the retry policy's own guard producing the exact failure the policy was written to remove. **Process-wide scoping is still right** and is unchanged: Vertex quota is enforced per project and region, so a parent plus three subagents holding four `Model()` handles must not be able to fire four independent retries into one shed. What a single timestamp gets wrong is the *shape* of the traffic, not the scope. A shed is by definition the condition under which several concurrent callers are rejected within seconds of each other, so 429s arrive **correlated by construction**, and a budget of one-per-window is spent by whoever arrives first and abandons the rest of the burst — which is the entire population of calls the feature exists for. `lastRetry time.Time` is therefore now a token bucket: **burst `models.RetryBurst` = 3, refill one per `Cooldown`**. The steady state is arithmetically identical to what it replaced, one extra request per window under sustained pressure, and past the burst a shed still degrades to plain pass-through; only the correlated moment is covered, at a worst case of three extra requests in a window rather than one. Three is the largest correlated burst the drill archive shows — a parent plus two subagents — and the bucket caps there however long the daemon has been idle, so nothing banks an unbounded rescue allowance. Note what this is **not** justified by: the suppression was observed once, and [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) explicitly declined to act on it for that reason. The n has not changed. What makes it actionable is that the correlation is structural rather than measured, and that the change's cost is bounded arithmetic rather than a behaviour anyone has to be right about. The process-wide test is kept and now earns its name harder: `TestTransientRetryBudgetIsProcessWide` spends the whole budget through three separate `builtinsLLM` wrappers and then watches a fresh one find it already gone, which a per-instance policy could not produce. The second defect is that **ten of the thirteen real retries in that batch logged no outcome at all**, which matters because the retry writes only to the daemon's stderr — it is invisible in the session transcript — and the provider docs tell operators to read these lines. The cause is that `Wrap` reported at the end of the happy path: the iterator has eight `return`s and six of them are a consumer that stopped reading mid-flush, which is the *ordinary* case, since the agent loop stops as soon as it has what it needs. Reporting moved into a `defer`, so every exit is covered, and the recovery is recorded the moment usable content exists — **before** the first `yield` — because by then the retry has already succeeded and whether the consumer goes on to read all of it is a different question. Two previously-silent outcomes are now named: a retry abandoned because the context died during the backoff, and a retry that returned nothing usable. Every retry that fires now logs exactly one of four outcome lines, so `retrying once` with nothing after it means the process died in between and nothing else — which retires the caveat the provider docs carried (*“the outcome line often does not appear”*). The suppression line is reworded to name the budget rather than a cooldown that no longer describes it. ([#1039](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1039))
- **A per-turn cost ceiling halted the whole session, and survived a pod roll doing it — so one expensive turn ended an unattended run permanently.** `--max-turn-cost-usd` is named for a turn and documented as the bound on a single runaway tool loop ([#144](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/144)), but a trip against it took the same path as a per-session trip: it set the session-wide `costCeilingExceeded` flag, so every subsequent turn was refused at pre-flight until an operator reset it, **and** it wrote the durable [#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643) guardrail row, so `RestoreGuardrails` re-armed the halt in the next process. That second half is the sharp one: a bound scoped to one turn produced a halt that outlived the pod that spent the money, and no amount of restarting cleared it. On an eight-hour overnight run the observable behaviour was a session that stopped at 02:00 over a single turn that cost eleven cents and reported nothing a client could see, because a refused turn emits no frame. A per-turn trip now ends its turn and nothing else — no flag, no durable row, no reset needed, and the next turn starts from a fresh per-turn baseline — which required the trip to get a once-per-turn latch of its own (`turnCeilingTripped`, cleared at each turn's start), since `costCeilingExceeded` had been quietly serving as that latch and the in-turn tap fires on every event of a turn whose cost is already over the bound. Taking that literally and stopping there would have been worse than the bug, though, and this is the part worth reading: a driver that re-drives — a wake loop, auto-continue — would then spend up to the ceiling per turn forever, turning a hard stop into an unbounded spend loop, which is precisely the shape the flag was there to prevent. So the *bound* stays turn-scoped and the *runaway* gets a session-scoped answer: three turns in a row that each end in a per-turn trip escalate to the ordinary session halt, durable and reset-clearable like any other, with a reason that says "three turns in a row" rather than blaming the last one. One turn that finishes inside its bound clears the count, so an agent that occasionally runs hot never reaches it, and an operator reset clears the count along with the halt — leaving it armed would re-halt on the next trip, one turn after the operator said carry on. Three is deliberately small: one trip is an expensive turn, two is a coincidence, three is a pattern nobody is watching. Nothing changes for `--max-session-cost-usd`, which measures an accumulator that does not reset and therefore still halts on the first trip. Every trip is still reported on the wire as a `cost_ceiling` `guardrail-trip`, so an attached operator sees the pattern build rather than only its conclusion; what changed is that the frame no longer implies the session is halted, and `GET /guardrails` (`halted`) remains the authoritative answer for a client that needs to branch. ([#1049](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1049))
- **Two daemons sharing one event log no longer answer the same interrupted question twice.** The run lock was doing less than its presence suggested: `lockClassifyInject` holds it across detection and injection, then releases it, and the continuation turn runs *afterwards*, asynchronously, in the session's wake loop. So the second daemon acquires the lock perfectly cleanly, reads a tail the first daemon's turn has not yet touched, and injects a second continuation into the same session. **That is not a race, and the issue's framing of it as one is the first thing this changes** — it reproduces sequentially, with the two passes strictly ordered and arbitrarily far apart, because nothing about daemon A's injection is observable to daemon B until A's turn commits an event. The same gap catches a *single* daemon whose `retry_interval` tick lands between its own inject and that note committing, which needs no fleet at all. The fix is therefore **not** the one [#977](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/977) scoped — "hold the lock for the duration of the continuation turn" — and that was rejected on the merits: mechanically there is no turn-end hook on this path to release it from, and semantically it would be worse than the bug, because ordinary turns take no lock at all (not attach-driven, not wake-loop, not the local REPL), so a lock held across the continuation would exclude another *continuation* and nothing else while reading like session-level mutual exclusion — an invariant a future reader would rely on and not have. What ships instead is a durable per-interruption claim: `agent_continuation_claim` (`pkg/eventlog/claim.go`) holds one row per session naming the interruption instant most recently claimed, written as a conditional upsert (`ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE … WHERE interrupted_at_unix_nano <> ? OR claimed_at_unix_nano < ?`) so that "did I win" is `RowsAffected > 0` in a single statement — every in-tree caller already holds the run lock, but a compare-and-set whose correctness *depends* on a lock taken somewhere else is the same class of quietly-false invariant, and one statement costs nothing to make honest. It is keyed on the interruption rather than the session, so a session interrupted again after a continuation ran is a new fact and is continued again; a per-session flag would have disabled the feature for that session permanently. Three placement decisions each have their own test. The claim is taken **after** the four stand-down guards (`TurnInFlight`, pending operator input, paused, guardrail-halted), because those are all facts about *this* process and claiming ahead of them would let one daemon's local pause consume the interruption and silence a healthy peer. A failed inject **releases** the claim immediately rather than stranding the session for the TTL. And a claim *write* that errors stands down and stays charged, like `acSkippedLockErr`, since injecting unclaimed is the single outcome the mechanism exists to prevent. Claims expire after ten minutes, which is not tidiness but the correctness half of the design: the continuation note lands on an **in-memory** inbox, so a pod killed between the inject and the wake loop draining it loses the note, leaves the tail byte-for-byte unchanged, and — nobody being on the other end of an unattended session — guarantees no newer interruption ever arrives to unstick it, so an eternal claim would end that session's autonomy for good. Ten minutes is far longer than a wake plus a model round trip, after which the committed note has moved the tail and the claim is moot anyway, and it adds no delay on the boot path, where `breakerWindow` already skips a session attempted inside the same ten minutes. `acSkippedClaimed` is refundable for the [#575](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/575) reason (nothing was attempted, and in a fleet every loser would otherwise burn the shared cumulative cap); `acSkippedClaimErr` is not. This closes continuation-against-continuation only — a peer daemon's *ordinary* turn still announces nothing to us, which stays open under the [#796](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/796) note in `docs/auto-continue-design.md`. ([#977](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/977))
- **One unreadable run in the drill archive no longer takes every other run with it.** `dev/trajectory`'s `LoadRuns` treated a directory it could not parse as fatal, on the stated reasoning that a run we cannot read is a finding about the recorder rather than a run to drop quietly. The reasoning is right and the consequence was not: the archive is append-only history, so a single directory damaged once permanently blocks every analysis of every run before *and* after it. That is not hypothetical — a `jq` failure inside `drill.sh` on 2026-09-13 truncated one run's `meta.json` to zero bytes, and from then on `LoadRuns` refused the whole corpus, both archive tests included, with a `unexpected end of JSON input` naming a file nobody was asking about. (The rig side is already fixed: `drill_write_meta` writes through a temp file and renames, so a failing `jq` leaves the previous `meta.json` alone instead of a truncated one.) `LoadRuns` now returns `([]*Trajectory, []SkippedRun, error)` — the casualties come back as data rather than as a log line the caller can forget to read, which is what keeps the original reasoning intact. The CLI prints each skip to stderr before anything else, so a reader piping the table or the JSON somewhere still learns the corpus is short; `TestAgreesWithScorePyOnTheArchive` logs them; and `TestDelegationMatchesTheArchive` **errors** on a skipped run that is pinned, because a pinned run going unreadable is a different animal from an archive being pruned and the existing shrinking-denominator guard cannot tell the two apart. A directory with no `transcript.jsonl` at all is still silently not-a-run: that is how you say "this is a notes folder", not "the recorder broke". ([#1045](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1045))
- **A session a guardrail has halted now goes inert instead of waking a driver every few minutes to be refused again.** Both pre-flights refuse at the top of `Agent.Run`, 45 lines above `drainInboxFull` — correct, because a refused turn must not eat the operator's message — but it means a queued inject is unreachable for as long as the halt stands, and an inject fires the wake signal, so every arriving message woke a driver that could only refuse. Observed live in the 2026-09-13 drill batch (`dev/uat/gke-drill/runs/2026-09-13-std-simian-test-bc-20run-post935.md`): three sessions cycling every 3–4 minutes, one of them **thirteen times over 51 minutes**, nine minutes after the last run finished, with nobody in reach of the cluster. It was invisible in all 59 transcripts and obvious in the daemon log, which is the second time in two days that has been the difference (`--timestamps`, see [#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935)). **Two corrections to the issue as filed, because both shaped the fix.** It cost *no model spend* — the pre-flights return before any model call, so "burning a model turn every three minutes" is wrong. And it *did* lose operator input, which the issue does not mention: the inbox drops the **oldest** message past `defaultInboxCap`, so a halted session that keeps receiving injects silently discards the earliest ones, and the loss grows with how long the halt stands. That rules out both obvious fixes — draining on refusal discards the very message the queue exists to protect, and letting the halt clear itself gives back the hard stop [#623](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/623)–[#628](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/628) earned from a live runaway. What changes instead is who gets woken: a halted agent still queues the inject, still emits its `inbox` frame, still wakes every **observer** (the local TUI keeps seeing input arrive), and still publishes `wake` — it just stops handing the **driver** a reason to call `Run`. The driver/observer split is already load-bearing ([#813](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/813)): `WakeRequested()` hands out the driver's subscription and `SubscribeWake()` an observer's, so the fence has a real seam to sit on rather than a new flag. The guardrail reset releases the fence, and the queued input drives the first post-reset turn, which is where it was always meant to land. `pkg/compose`'s auto-continue stands down on the same predicate rather than queueing a continuation note nobody can deliver — calling `RestoreGuardrails` first, because the path that reaches it on a restarted pod has run no turn and the in-memory flags are still false, so a guard that only read them would be blind on exactly the case it was written for. Two limits worth stating: a refused turn emits no frame at all, so neither the old refusal nor the new fence reaches an attached operator — both land in the daemon log, one line per distinct halt rather than one per fenced wake — and surfacing a standing halt to an operator is [#891](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/891). A driver that calls `Run` on its own schedule rather than on a wake (the autonomous scheduler's sleep timer) still reaches the pre-flight and is still refused, cheaply and now legibly. ([#1040](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1040))
- **A talkative subagent no longer costs the GKE drill an entire run on the way to disk.** `20260913T110018Z-c` had already streamed and written 36 frames when it died with `jq: Argument list too long`, and the diagnosis that reads naturally off that message is the wrong one. The cap is **`MAX_ARG_STRLEN`, 128 KiB** — per *argument*, not per command line, nothing to do with `ARG_MAX`, and not raisable with `ulimit`; confirmed against `/bin/true` at 131000 bytes (fine) and 131073 (fails). `drill_capture_subagents` carried the whole merged event array through a single `jq --argjson`, and captures in this recipe run 50–60 KiB, so the margin was about 2× and a chatty RBAC run crossed it. The accumulator now goes through a file with `--slurpfile`, which has no such limit. The second half is why one lost artifact became one lost run: `drill_write_meta` redirected straight into `meta.json`, so the shell truncated the file *before* `jq` ran and the failure left **0 bytes** behind — and `score.py` raises `JSONDecodeError` on an empty `meta.json` rather than rendering the partial sheet the cleanup trap exists to produce, so the salvage path that was supposed to make this cheap is exactly what the bug disabled. Both writes now go to a temp and `mv`. The regression test is behavioural rather than structural: `dryrun.sh 14` pads a fixture subagent past the cap and drives the real `drill.sh` end to end, asserting the frames survive, that the fixture genuinely clears 131072 bytes (a test that cannot exercise the bug is worse than none), that `meta.json` parses, and that the sheet rendered. Reverted against the pre-fix scripts it reproduces the live failure exactly — exit 126, zero frames, 0-byte `meta.json`, no sheet. Found by the post-[#935](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/935) verification batch, whose note records it: `dev/uat/gke-drill/runs/2026-09-13-std-simian-test-bc-20run-post935.md`.
- **`dev/release/cut-ga-tag.sh` no longer exits non-zero on a fold that worked.** The script printed a truncated preview of the CHANGELOG it had just rewritten with `git --no-pager diff -- "$CHANGELOG" | head -60`. Under `set -o pipefail` and `set -e` that combination is a trap: `head` exits once it has its 60 lines, `git` takes a SIGPIPE on the next write, `141` becomes the pipeline's status, and the script stops there — *after* the fold is already on disk. It failed having succeeded, and everything printed below the preview went missing: the `EDITORIAL PASS REQUIRED` notice and the next-steps block, which are the only instructions the operator gets for the one step the script deliberately does not perform (replacing the `<HEADLINE>` placeholder, then committing and tagging). v2.9.0 was cut twice through a script exiting 141. The diff is now captured into a variable and truncated from a herestring, which has no writer for `head` to kill; `|| true` would have masked the 141 too, but it masks a real `git` failure along with it, and that point is the last place a broken CHANGELOG is cheap to notice. The regression case lives in `dev/tools/verify-release-notes` rather than in a new harness, because `cut-ga-tag.sh` writes the section `compose-release-notes.sh` later turns into a release body — the two scripts are one pipeline, and one assertion does not earn a second CI job. Worth recording for anyone who tries to reproduce it: the precondition is the **pipe buffer**, not the 60-line window. `head` does not close the pipe until it has read its 60 lines and exited, so a diff small enough to sit in the buffer is written whole and `git` is gone before there is anything left to signal — a 216-line fixture of short bullets exits `0`, and the same 216 lines of paragraph-length bullets is 80 KiB and reproduces it every time. That is also why the real thing fired on every GA fold: this repo writes CHANGELOG entries as one-line paragraphs. ([#1024](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1024))

#### Other (Cleanup)
- **The `gke-deploy` example's agentic small model is now `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`, not `gemini-2.5-flash`.** It matches the provider's own cheap-tier default and is pinned explicitly. LiteLLM marked the Gemini 2.5 models deprecated on 2026-10-20. They still answer, and core-models keeps their prices for 90 days past that date, but once the rows drop, a cost ceiling would refuse them as unpriced. Overlays that set `AGENTIC_SMALL_MODEL` themselves are unaffected. ([#1337](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1337))
- **core-models v0.13.1** (from v0.8.0). Beyond pricing: the `openai-chat` dialect now refuses a generation setting it can't send (top-k, logprobs, safety settings, cached content, non-text modalities, labels, …) instead of dropping it; core-agent sets none of them on profile requests, so nothing changes in practice. xAI reasoning replies now count their answer tokens. No SDK floor moves. ([#1328](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1328))
- **core-models v0.8.0: the Gemini grounding projection and context-cache hooks now come from the library.** `cmd/core-agent` wires core-models' `adkv2.GroundingProjection`; `gemini.GroundingProjection` stays as a deprecated wrapper over it, with identical rows (`gemini/google_search`, same text, same dedup). Installing Vertex context-cache hooks no longer builds a second Gemini client: the hooks go onto the one client in place (`Client.SetContextCache`), so the cache manager's `genai.Caches` and every model share it. Claude-on-Vertex errors now name the real `:streamRawPredict` URL. ([#1320](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1320))
- **The harness config-pin gate no longer lets a comment's apostrophes decide what it finds.** Two lexer defects in `dev/harness-config-check`, both found when a three-apostrophe comment in `dev/uat/self-dev/run.sh` made two correctly pinned invocations report as unpinned. First, double quotes didn't open a fresh context at `$(`, so in `"$(cmd "arg")"` the outer string closed at the inner quote. Second, and the larger: the top-level heredoc detector matched a `<<` *inside* a word's substitution (`X="$(python3 - <<'PY' … PY\n)"`), registered a heredoc whose delimiter could never match, and swallowed the rest of the file as its body. `run.sh` had passed only because its later apostrophes happened to balance. The scanner now skips a balanced `$( … )` aware of quotes, nested substitutions, heredoc bodies, comments, `$'…'` and herestrings, and finds a word's heredoc operator by walking its quoting, so `cat >"$f"<<EOF` is still a heredoc and a `<<` inside a substitution is not a top-level one. Pinned four ways: new bash-oracle cases (one of which showed the skew dropping an invocation from the scan entirely — the fatal direction for a violation scanner); pin-credit cases for each construct, because the oracle counts invocations and the damage is to pins; a token-level case; and a property over the live tree — an apostrophe added to every comment line of every scanned script must change no finding, with `bash -n` deciding which `#` lines are really comments. ([#1209](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1209))
- **core-agent no longer uses the two APIs ADK v1.7.0 deprecated.** `tool.Context` is now `agent.ToolContext`, its target type, across 88 files, with no behaviour change. `session.NewEvent` is now `session.NewEventWithContext`, which honours a platform time or UUID provider on the context: the two call sites that have a request context pass it, and the rest pass `context.Background()`. The lint exclusion that held the deprecations back is deleted, so a new use of either fails lint again. The library-embedding skill's custom-tool example now uses `agent.ToolContext` too, so an embedder who copies it doesn't trip the same deprecation. ([#1193](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1193))
- **A required `agent attribution` check now rejects AI-agent attribution in a PR's commits, title and body.** Claude was on the repository's contributor list because of one commit, `e385fb04`: a branch commit on #931 carried an agent `Co-Authored-By` trailer, and the squash merge copied it into `main`. `AGENTS.md` already banned the trailer, but nothing enforced it. `dev/ci/presubmits/verify-no-agent-attribution` scans *every* commit on the branch, because a squash merge copies each branch commit's co-authors into `main`, so cleaning the PR body alone does nothing. It reads every line of each message rather than git's parsed trailers, because a squash can push a trailer out of the last paragraph, where git stops seeing it but GitHub still counts it. It rejects an agent co-author, a "Generated with" footer, an agent as commit author or committer, and the self-development rig's `Self-Development-Run:` trailer, which was agent attribution under another name. The rig now requires the *absence* of any agent marker (A8 and A10 run the same scanner), and which PR a run opened is recorded in the run's own scorecard. A human co-author is legitimate, so a credit trailer fails on the *identity* it credits (an agent's noreply or GitHub App address, or a display name that is wholly an agent product name), never on a substring: this repository's commit bodies discuss Claude and Gemini models constantly, and humans are called Devin and Jules. A footer fails only when the line *is* the footer, behind any markdown prefix. CI runs the base branch's copy of the scanner, so a PR can't switch the check off by editing it, and it scans the merge ref's `HEAD^1..HEAD^2` rather than the event's base SHA, which can lag. An unresolvable range exits 2 instead of scanning nothing and passing. Run over all of `main`'s history, it flags exactly the two known commits and nothing else, and over the last 300 PR bodies it flags nothing. History is deliberately not rewritten: removing `e385fb04`'s trailer would re-SHA 129 commits and move the published v2.9.0 tag. A second workflow re-scans each push to `main`, plus a daily sweep of the last three days for pushes that skipped CI, and opens an issue on a hit. It catches what the PR check never judged: an `--admin` merge that didn't wait for the check, a direct push, or a squash message edited at merge time. ([#1149](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1149))
- **Every harness script that runs the binary now pins its config with `-c`, so a `.agents/` at the repo root cannot silently re-parent the test suite — and the mitigation the design doc prescribed for this turned out not to work.** `config.Find` walks *up* from the process cwd and returns the first `.agents/` it meets, which means an unpinned invocation anywhere under the checkout inherits whatever config sits above it, with no error and no output: a different model, a different permission mode, a `tools.disable` list that removes the tool under test. `dev/smoke/05-headless-gate.sh` exists to test bash gating and would have gone on passing while testing nothing. This is not hypothetical, and it is not a latent risk either — it was found by pointing the check at a developer machine where an untracked `/.agents/` was already in place, and thirteen exec sites were reading it: eleven across nine `dev/smoke/*.sh` scripts and two command strings that `dev/uat/attach/run.sh` builds and dispatches through tmux. On that machine an unpinned run did not merely pick up somebody else's model; it **failed**, because the root `AGENTS.md` uses a session-state placeholder a smoke run does not set — `failed to append instructions: failed to inject session state into instruction: state key does not exist`, after `instruction: loaded 2 file(s)`. The pin also moves the content root (`agentsDir` defaults to `Dir(-c)`), so the six scripts pinned at the pristine `/tmp` config no longer read the checkout's own `AGENTS.md` into the model's system prompt, `record_plan` becomes registered and the persisted-grant store stops being a no-op. That is the intended direction rather than a side effect — a smoke test should not be running against the contributor guide — but it is a behaviour change and `dev/smoke/_common.sh` says so where the next reader will be. The smoke scripts pin a pristine `${SMOKE_CONFIG}` written under `/tmp` by a new `pristine_config` helper in `_common.sh`; the three scripts that already write a config of their own pin *that*, so the pin names a file the script controls rather than a second source of truth. **`--agents-dir` is not a pin, which is the correction.** [#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116)'s D3 prescribed it, and `loadConfig()` reads `config.json` by discovery from the cwd *before* `resolveAgentsDir()` applies the flag — so `--agents-dir` relocates the skills, MCP servers, env file and sessions and leaves the model, permissions and budgets exactly where they were. `splitTreeWarning()` in `cmd/core-agent/agents_dir.go` exists to warn about that split, and it was warning about it correctly the whole time. Verified live rather than argued from the source: with `--agents-dir` pointed at an empty tree the startup summary still read `config: source=<repo>/.agents/config.json` and the repo config's model; with `-c` it read the pinned file. The design doc is corrected in place rather than quietly, because a mitigation that does not mitigate is worse than a known gap. The gate is `dev/ci/presubmits/verify-harness-config-pinned` (tool: `dev/harness-config-check`), a step in the existing required `test` job rather than a new check name for branch protection to learn. **It scans what the scripts execute, not what they contain**, and that distinction is the whole reason it needed a lexer: the two attach-UAT sites are real invocations living inside double-quoted strings, so a grep for `-c` on the same line is wrong in both directions, and the `shellCode()` helper `examples/gke-platform-agent/recipe_test.go` already ships blanks quoted spans and would be blind to exactly them. The soundness direction is the inverse of that check's — this one fails when it *finds* something, so a miss is fatal and a false positive is merely loud, and every ambiguity resolves towards "treat it as code". Hence descent into `"…"`, `'…'`, `$(…)`, backticks and heredoc bodies, and hence the lexer's own test being a **bash oracle**: every case is executed through real `bash` with a sentinel-printing stub on `PATH`, and the scanner's count must equal the execution count, with the two recorded divergences argued one at a time (`<<'EOF'` scans 1 and executes 0 — the safe direction, since `bash <<'EOF'` with that body would run it). A companion test asserts an exact number of cases reach the stub, so the oracle cannot pass by executing nothing. The gate is mutation-verified against the real tree, not a fixture: every pinned site is un-pinned one at a time in the actual script text and must come back as a finding on the right line, and the mutation fails loudly if it changed nothing — a pin the scanner credits to a line that does not carry it would otherwise read as thirteen passing subtests. `TestInvocationCensus` asserts the per-file count for the same reason `--print` exists: a violation scanner is silent about the site it has stopped recognising, so only a number notices when one goes missing. `attach` and `ls` are exempt structurally rather than by grant — both are dispatched in `main()` before `flag.Parse`, read no config, and reject `-c` outright. **Deciding a site is *pinned* needed the opposite bias, which the first draft did not have**, and the adversarial review is what found it: a `-c` credited in error is as silent a pass as a missed invocation, so inside a dispatched command string — where the lexer splits on whitespace and an argument's words become tokens — `-p 'explain the -c flag'` marked the site pinned forever, in exactly the two sites the check was built for. A `-c` the quote scan places inside a quote no longer counts, and the value has to look like a config path, which also stops a `-c` that is really the value of the flag before it. The same review found `\"${BIN}\"` invisible (the backslash survived `unquote`, so the invocation vanished rather than going unpinned — the fatal direction) and one-line `{ …; }` brace groups dropping command position; both are fixed and both are now bash-oracle cases, which is why the oracle is 37 cases and 19 executions rather than 34 and 16. What the gate does **not** claim is written down rather than implied: `-c` does not close `~/.agents/AGENTS.md`, and `eval` / `xargs` / `find -exec` / `set -- … "$@"` / variable indirection remain out of a lexer's reach. ([#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116))
- **The shim called a healthy pod "waiting", and `get events -o json` was still the text table.** Both found by the live re-run that [#1123](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1123) unblocked, and both the same class it fixed: not a shrug, a confident answer that is false. `describe` hardcoded `State: Waiting` and `Ready: False`, so a pod the world declares `Running`, `1/1`, `0 restarts` was reported as *waiting, because it is running* — and disagreed with its own `-o json`, which derived readiness from the world correctly. The run noticed and spent turns deciding whether the **cluster** was inconsistent, in a case whose entire subject is what survives a long horizon, which makes the instrument the worst possible thing to spend the horizon on. State and readiness now come from one helper both surfaces call, since the defect was two hardcoded answers that were not the same answer; a running container gets `state.running` and no waiting reason, and a broken one still names the reason it is waiting for. Separately, `get events` never reached the structured renderer, so the silent-table fallback removed everywhere else in #1123 was still live for the kind an agent is most likely to pipe into `jq` — it survived because every output-format test exercised `get deployment`. Fixed, and generalised: the format tests are now driven by a per-kind table with a coverage contract against the shim's own `KIND_ALIASES`, so a kind the shim serves but nobody renders fails offline rather than waiting for a live run to trip over it. [#1128](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1128)
- **Three doc comments told the reader to call `agent.WithAttachPromptBroker`, which [#388](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/388)'s phase-4 split renamed to `attachadapter.WithPromptBroker` eight releases ago.** The costly one is on `pkg/attach.PromptBroker` itself — the type a reader lands on when they are working out how to turn the 501'ing `/perms` routes on — and the other two are in `internal/coretuiremote` and `internal/attachclient`, each explaining a 501 by naming the option that would have prevented it. All three were inherited by a port into the sibling mast repo, where the same text pointed at a symbol that does not exist there either ([mast#364](https://github.com/go-steer/mast/issues/364)). **Three, not the four the issue lists**: `attachadapter.WithPromptBroker`'s own doc comment reads *"Formerly agent.WithAttachPromptBroker."*, which is the rename-record convention the nine moved options in that file already share and is correct as written — it was miscounted because the sentence wraps, and the same wrap hid a tenth record on `WithSkillsProvider` that the issue missed in the other direction. `CHANGELOG.md`'s reference stays as the historical record of what shipped under that name. The guard is **not** the symbol-resolving doc lint #1103 weighed and declined; it checks one mechanical invariant over Go comments tree-wide — the string `WithAttach` may appear only as a record of the old name (`Formerly agent.WithAttachX.`, or the bare `WithAttach*` glob the package doc uses for the family), never as an instruction to call one, because no symbol of that name survives. Two details are load-bearing and both are pinned by mutation: comment groups are **collapsed to one line before matching**, since the convention wraps and two of the ten legitimate records are split across lines — matching line-by-line reports them as violations and the convention becomes unusable; and the check fails rather than passes when the marker matches nothing at all, so a walk that silently stops finding files is a failure instead of a green run. The test exempts its own file by compiled-in path rather than by name, since it is the one place that has to spell the banned forms out in order to define them. ([#1103](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1103))
- **The model-facing coverage sweep pruned dot-directories by name, so the next checkout parked under one broke the suite again.** `.claude/worktrees/<name>/` is a **full second checkout** of this repo, so a walk that descends into it grades another branch's source against this tree's sweep files: the 2026-09-04 analysis saw two test failures on a machine with 26 worktrees present, against a tree that passes `go test ./...` from a clean shallow clone. `.claude` had been added to a four-name skip list after it broke something, which left the rule describing known offenders rather than the class — and a suite that fails on the developer's untracked working state teaches people to ignore the suite, which costs more than any false negative it catches. Every dot-directory is now pruned, on the reasoning that nothing the module builds or ships lives in one; `node_modules` and `vendor` stay excluded by name because they are somebody else's code rather than working state. **The rule moved to one shared `testutil.PruneWalkDir`** rather than being fixed in place, because [#1103](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1103) had just added a second source walk with its own copy of the same four-name list — two copies of a rule that had already drifted once is how it drifts again — and both walks now read it, along with the `RepoRoot` helper they had each grown separately. **The walk root is exempt however it is named**, which is not hypothetical here: a checkout can live anywhere, this repo's own worktrees live under a dot-directory, and a walk that pruned its own root would find nothing and report a clean tree. The test plants that case rather than describing it — the synthetic root is itself dot-named, so the exception is covered by every assertion rather than by a case of its own — and plants a registering package inside `.claude/worktrees/`, `.fakeworktree/`, `node_modules/` and `vendor/`, asserting none is seen. It is planted rather than asserted against the real tree for the reason the bug is hard to hold onto: the directory that must not be walked is either absent (CI) or untracked (a developer machine), so a test reading the real root proves nothing where it matters and passes vacuously where it does not. A first assertion requires the planted *real* package to have been found, since a test whose only claim is an absence passes just as well when the walk is broken end to end — which is exactly what two of the six mutations do, and that guard is what catches them. ([#964](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/964))
- **`google.golang.org/grpc` v1.82.1 → v1.83.1, clearing GO-2026-6348.** The advisory is a heap-exhaustion OOM reachable through HTTP/2 DATA-frame fragmentation, and `govulncheck` puts it in the *symbol* results rather than the import-only bucket: the example traces run through `attach.Server.Serve` and `attach.broadcaster.Close`, so the reachable path is the attach hub's own listener — the long-lived, network-facing surface a daemon leaves open for the TUI. grpc arrives here only as an indirect dependency (ADK and the Google API clients pull it in; this repo imports no gRPC package itself), which is precisely why it went unnoticed until the advisory landed and turned a routine transitive pin into a red build on every open PR. The bump carries two transitive companions `go mod tidy` selects with it (`opentelemetry-operations-go/detectors/gcp` v1.32.0 → v1.33.0, `contrib/detectors/gcp` v1.43.0 → v1.44.0). No source change; `dev/ci/presubmits/verify-vuln` goes from one called vulnerability to zero.
- **`Agent.Run`'s pre-turn sequence is now a list you can read, and a list a test can permute.** Everything `Run` did before handing a request to the runner was an implicit contract held in two unenforceable places — the physical order of the statements, and a trail of comments naming the bugs each position prevented ([#362](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/362), [#145](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/145), [#144](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/144), [#623](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/623), [#537](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/537), and since then [#655](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/655)). Six defects at one seam is a structural signal rather than a style one, and it has nothing to do with length: a twenty-line version of the same implicit ordering would be exactly as dangerous, because nothing but production detects a transposed pair. The sequence is now a slice of named steps in `pkg/agent/preturn.go`, each carrying the constraint its position encodes, and `preturn_test.go` runs the pipeline against deliberately permuted copies — so every one of those bugs has a test that reproduces it on demand and fails if the steps ever move back. Behaviour is unchanged; what changed is that the ordering can no longer be lost. `Run` itself went from 129 lines / 57 statements to 102 / 40 as a side effect, which turned the [#662](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/662) complexity ratchet one notch (`funlen` statements 145 → 144) and re-measured the other two against today's tree ([#659](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/659)).

#### Documentation
- **The `presubmit-sweep` skill now says its loop is one compound command.** A permission gate's prefix rules never match a compound command, so under an approver the loop goes to a person. The skill now says how to run the scripts as separate calls, by path. ([#1341](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1341))
- **AGENTS.md and the release process now spell out the major-bump order.** The module path moves to `/vN` before any `vN` tag, pre-releases included, because a tag cut first can never be `go install`ed. `docs/release-process.md` lists every place the path lives and the post-tag `go install` check. ([#1312](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1312))
- **The TUI reference now says what `/subagents` lists.** It said `/subagents` showed the configured roster from `GET /subagents`. In both TUIs it lists the spawned instances from `GET /agents`, and `/subagents <name>` opens one. ([#1283](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1283))
- **`pkg/eventlog` now states the three things a non-SQL event log will not promise, before 3.0 freezes them.** The `Stream` contract never said what `Entry.Seq` means, that a query without `ForSession` or `WithSessionTree` (one that reads every session in the log) must always be served, or that `Handle.DB` is always set. The SQL implementation does all three, and code written against it could reasonably assume all three. `docs/external-state-design.md` plans Agent Engine and Redis event-log backends for 3.1 or later, and neither can. So the contract now says `Seq` is unique and increasing within one root session tree only (the part of a session ID before its first `:sub:`, so `<sid>:digest` is its own root); a query that names no session ID may be refused with an error wrapping the new `eventlog.ErrUnscopedQuery`, and an empty ID passed to `ForSession` or `WithSessionTree` counts as naming none; and `Handle.DB` is nil on a Handle with no SQL connection, which means "nothing to share", not "closed". One runtime fix rides along: `WithSessionTree` passed the parent ID into SQL `LIKE` unescaped, so a `_` or `%` in a session ID pulled in rows from other trees; it is escaped now. Otherwise nothing changes at runtime: the SQL implementation still assigns one global sequence, still serves unscoped queries (a new test pins that), and still sets `DB`. If you read across sessions or compare seqs from different sessions, handle `ErrUnscopedQuery` now and you are ready for those backends. ([#1192](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1192))
- **The self-development recipe's review gate can now finish a review, and the record of it has to say who found what.** In the first attended run the `reviewer` subagent declared no budget. Each of its three `spawn_agent` delegations fell back to the background default of $1, 50 turns and 10 minutes, and none of them got as far as a report. The author found all five defects alone, and the PR body credited the reviewer. The recipe now gives the reviewer $5, 150 turns and 30 minutes, and a recipe test holds each dimension above the default. The `adversarial-review-gate` skill now says to stage by name (never `git add -A`), to delegate again when a reviewer comes back without a report, and to credit each finding to whoever made it. The reviewer's persona used to tell it to mutate the tree and restore it; that tree is the author's working tree, and in that run a reviewer's mutation collided with the author's own. It now mutates a scratch copy. ([#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116))
- **The mouse-selection docs no longer tell VS Code users to hold Alt.** Four site pages and the `core-agent-tui --no-mouse` help said the bypass for mouse capture in VS Code's integrated terminal was "Alt/Option". xterm.js has no Alt bypass on any platform: it is Shift-drag, or Option-drag on macOS, where it also needs `terminal.integrated.macOptionClickForcesSelection`, which is off by default and which a workspace `.vscode/settings.json` overrides. VS Code picks between them by the machine running the editor UI, not the host core-agent runs on, which the `/mouse` section of the slash-command reference now explains. The capture hint itself named Alt off a macOS host until core-agent took a core-tui release with [go-steer/core-tui#317](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/pull/317); the v0.27.0 bump below carries it, and the caveat is gone from the page. ([#1156](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/1156))
- **`AGENTS.md` now says which doc changes get a CHANGELOG bullet.** It used to list "doc" among the user-visible changes with no qualifier, so read literally every doc change needed a bullet, and practice almost never did it: 7 of the 99 docs-only commits on main carried one. The first self-development run followed the text and wrote a bullet for its site page, and its grader, which had encoded the practice, failed it. The new rule, in `AGENTS.md` ("Which doc changes are user-visible") and the `changelog-bullet` skill: the published site under `docs/site/src/content/docs/`, `README.md`, the READMEs under `examples/`, and a change to a *rule* in `AGENTS.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `docs/release-process.md` or an `.agents/` skill get a bullet under `#### Documentation`. Everything else under `docs/` and `dev/` is internal and gets none; when an internal doc records a user-visible change, the bullet belongs to that change. This narrows the old text for internal docs and changes practice for the published site. "Bullet every doc change" was rejected because design notes and UAT write-ups would bury the release notes; "never bullet docs" because site readers and contributors do need to hear about changes to what they work from. Past entries are not backfilled or edited, including the design-doc bullet below. This also corrects the record in `dev/uat/self-dev/README.md`: it said the recipe *required* the bullet, which the text supported, without saying that practice had never followed it. Separately, from T1 up every self-development task file names the issue it resolves, because a task that names none leaves the agent citing the epic. ([#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116))
- **The configuration reference documented the `.agents/` walk-up as a convenience and never as a hazard, so the one failure it causes was undiscoverable from the page that causes it.** `config.Find` walks up from the process cwd and takes the first hit, with no error, no prompt and no boundary other than that first match — so a run started anywhere under a project that carries a `.agents/` at its root silently adopts that project's model, permission mode, cost ceilings and tool surface, and behaves like a different agent while saying nothing. The page described the walk-up under "Discovery and merge" and documented `-c` beside it as an override, which reads as a preference rather than as the fix for a silent inheritance. **The hazard is now stated where the walk-up is**, as its consequence: what the reader would observe, the startup summary's `config: source=… (via .agents/ discovery)` line as the way to see which config actually loaded, and `-c <path>` as the pin for any run that must not inherit. The `--agents-dir` disclaimer is the part that is not obvious from the precedence table two paragraphs down and is the reason the section exists in prose: the config is loaded by discovery *before* that flag is applied, so reaching for it moves the skills, plans and content roots and leaves the model, permissions and budgets behind — the half that decides what the agent may do. Deliberately **not** a restructure of the page and deliberately **not** a new runtime check: the harness-side enforcement already exists (`dev/ci/presubmits/verify-harness-config-pinned`), and what was missing was the sentence a human hunting this behaviour could find. ([#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116))
- **`docs/self-development-design.md` — pointing core-agent at core-agent, with the feasibility question answered by measurement rather than argument.** The doc settles where the artifacts live (a committed `/.agents/` recipe, five artifact classes, and the Go gotcha that keeps the recipe's test at `internal/selfrecipe/` because the toolchain ignores dot-directories), defines a T0→T3 ladder, and records the hazard that fixes the build order: committing `/.agents/` silently re-parents *every* unpinned harness invocation, because `config.Find` walks up and takes the first hit. That is why P2 — pinning the harness scripts and adding `verify-agents-dir-pinned` — has to land before the recipe exists. It also carries a four-arm spike on one real task drawn from P2 itself, graded by a fixed six-mutation rubric: core-agent driving `claude-opus-5` scored 5/6, identical to Claude Code driving the same model and missing the identical case, while `claude-opus-4-5` scored 1/6 and 2/6. The harness is not the limiting factor; the model tier is, and the recipe pins a frontier model as a requirement rather than a default. Two findings are recorded against the plan rather than resolved by it: both core-agent runs died mid-task with `anthropic: stream: context canceled` under no external timeout and no budget or watchdog trip, which gates T3 specifically; and the two strongest arms agree exactly on `dev/smoke` (11 unpinned invocations in 9 scripts) while splitting on `dev/uat/attach/run.sh`, where the binary is assembled into a string and handed to tmux — one flags it, the other says in its source that a shell scanner cannot follow it. Both calls are defensible, and the split turns out to be the same question the corrected scanner convention asks, which is why P2 writes the check by hand with that disagreement as a known test case. ([#1116](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/1116))
